### 2.1 Dictionary: CC-CEDICT

**What it is.** CC-CEDICT is a community-maintained Chinese-English dictionary published by MDBG. Its file header names the official page, [mdbg.net/chinese/dictionary?page=cc-cedict](https://www.mdbg.net/chinese/dictionary?page=cc-cedict), an online editor for corrections and a project wiki ([release file of 2026-05-18](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)). The release the app uses is dated 2026-05-18T07:15:15Z and has 124,933 entries ([release file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)).

**Licence.** The header states the licence twice, in words and as the machine-readable line `#! license=https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/`: Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike 4.0 International ([CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/)). It also carries "Published by MDBG" and "CEDICT - Copyright (C) 1997, 1998 Paul Andrew Denisowski" ([release file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)). Two npm packages that ship the data say "MIT" in their package metadata. That covers only their code, as one of them says in its README, so the dictionary header governs ([@leonsilicon/cc-cedict](https://registry.npmjs.org/@leonsilicon/cc-cedict), [cc-cedict 1.1.1](https://registry.npmjs.org/cc-cedict/-/cc-cedict-1.1.1.tgz)).

**Attribution.** Anyone who shares the data, changed or not, must keep the creator and copyright notices supplied with it, link to the material where practical, say that it was changed, and name the licence with its link; a link to a page that holds this information is enough ([CC BY-SA 4.0 legal code, §3(a)](https://github.com/creativecommons/cc-legal-tools-data/blob/a0dac18d1a773b4cdb7d8dd5d9bf4efab7901030/docs/licenses/by-sa/4.0/legalcode.en.html)). The header contains no warranty disclaimer, so there is none to pass on ([release file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)).

ShareAlike reaches files built from CC-CEDICT, such as the app's word files with their English meanings: they must be shared under CC BY-SA 4.0 (or a later version) without extra restrictions. Because the EU, including the Netherlands, also protects databases as such, the licence asks for the credit whenever all or a substantial part of the entries is shared. ShareAlike does not reach the app's code or content written independently and kept in separate files ([legal code §1, §3(b), §4](https://github.com/creativecommons/cc-legal-tools-data/blob/a0dac18d1a773b4cdb7d8dd5d9bf4efab7901030/docs/licenses/by-sa/4.0/legalcode.en.html)). Applying the licence this way is an interpretation (medium confidence), not legal advice. The research proposes this credit text; MDBG prescribes no wording:

> Dictionary data: CC-CEDICT (community-maintained free Chinese-English dictionary), published by MDBG, release 2026-05-18 - https://www.mdbg.net/chinese/dictionary?page=cc-cedict - licensed under CC BY-SA 4.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/). Includes CEDICT, Copyright (C) 1997, 1998 Paul Andrew Denisowski. Modified for this app: subset of entries, pinyin converted from tone numbers to tone marks, glosses shortened.

**File format.** A UTF-8 text file: 21 comment lines, 9 metadata lines (`#! entries=124933`, `#! date=…`, `#! license=…` and others), then one entry per line in the form `Traditional Simplified [pin1 yin1] /meaning 1/meaning 2/`. Lines end in Windows line breaks (CRLF), which a parser must strip. Inside the meanings, measure words are written `CL:個|个[ge4]`, and notes such as "variant of", "also pr. […]" (also pronounced) and "Taiwan pr. […]" point to other forms and readings ([release file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)).

**Pinyin conventions.** CC-CEDICT does not write pinyin the way a learner reads it, so the app converts it ([release file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)):

| CC-CEDICT writes | Example | The app shows |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Tone numbers 1-4, and 5 for the neutral tone (4,716 syllables) | 吗 ma5, 衣服 yi1 fu5 | tone marks; the neutral tone unmarked (ma, yīfu) |
| ü as "u:" (1,228 syllables) | 女 nu:3 | nǚ |
| Erhua as a separate syllable "r5" (692 syllables) | 一点儿 yi1 dian3 r5 | yīdiǎnr |
| Syllables separated by spaces, no apostrophes | 西安 Xi1 an1 | one word, with an apostrophe where needed (Xī'ān) |
| A capital for proper nouns, also in 星期一 Xing1 qi1 yi1 (20,680 entries start with one) | 北京 Bei3 jing1 | HSK words keep the HSK list's spelling (中国 Zhōngguó, 星期天 xīngqītiān); cross-references in meanings are shown in lower case |
| Dictionary (citation) tones only; tone changes are never written | 一起 yi1 qi3, 不对 bu4 dui4 | citation tones, plus a hint of the spoken form (2.6) |
| Each reading in its own entry, in an order that does not show which is common (2,298 of 121,159 simplified headwords have more than one) | 吗 ma2, ma3, ma5 | the reading chosen by meaning |

**Compared with the HSK lists.** Of the 11,049 rows of the HSK 3.0 (2021) list that could be compared (11,092 less 43 written with variant notation), 98.2% match a CC-CEDICT reading exactly; 33 differ only in capital letters, 138 in a tone or neutral tone, 5 in a syllable, and 27 are not CC-CEDICT headwords ([CC-CEDICT release](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt), [ivankra/hsk30 list](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ivankra/hsk30/master/hsk30.csv)). Neutral tones differ in both directions: the HSK list has 别人 biérén where CC-CEDICT has bie2 ren5, and 北边 běibian where CC-CEDICT has bei3 bian1. Where the HSK list has another syllable (谁 shéi/shuí, 熟 shú/shóu), CC-CEDICT usually records it as an "also pr." note in the meaning, not in the pinyin field (same sources). CC-CEDICT, the HSK list and the HSK website each follow their own neutral-tone policy; the app keeps the exam list's reading (except three apparent misprints in the list, where it uses CC-CEDICT's) and records every difference with a decision (section 1 and [data/hsk/resolutions.json](../data/hsk/resolutions.json)).

**Releases.** MDBG regenerates the file about once a day: a GitHub mirror that downloads it daily logged a median gap of 0.99 days between releases from March to October 2026 ([rhcarvalho/cedict mirror](https://github.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/tree/55a0cf1226b044c59579dd8e0a32114ca6a14a11)). The first research answer said "at least weekly", inferred from a weekly npm job; the verifier corrected it from the mirror's daily log, which is the figure used here. Releases change materially: between 2026-05-18 and 2026-10-31 (125,346 entries), 1,121 entries were removed, 1,534 added and 1,890 meanings changed, touching 291 HSK 3.0 headwords ([mirror](https://github.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/tree/55a0cf1226b044c59579dd8e0a32114ca6a14a11), [release of 2026-10-31](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/55a0cf1226b044c59579dd8e0a32114ca6a14a11/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)). The download address can also serve old files: on 2026-03-03 it returned a release from 2017 with 114,959 entries, and 25 daily downloads since 2022 returned an older release than the one before ([mirror log](https://github.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/tree/55a0cf1226b044c59579dd8e0a32114ca6a14a11)).

**How the pipeline obtains it.** From the environment where the data was built, mdbg.net answered HTTP 403, so [the MDBG export](https://www.mdbg.net/chinese/export/cedict/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt.gz) could not be downloaded. [scripts/fetch-sources.mjs](../scripts/fetch-sources.mjs) instead downloads the npm package @leonsilicon/cc-cedict 0.0.0 as a file ([tarball](https://registry.npmjs.org/@leonsilicon/cc-cedict/-/cc-cedict-0.0.0.tgz)), without running `npm install`, so no install script runs. It extracts the package's JSON copy of the dictionary and checks its SHA-256 against [data/sources.lock.json](../data/sources.lock.json).

That package is a lossless copy of the MDBG release of 2026-05-18: its 124,933 entries equal the raw file field by field and it keeps the header ([npm entry](https://registry.npmjs.org/@leonsilicon/cc-cedict)). An independent daily mirror holds a byte-identical copy of the same release ([rhcarvalho/cedict, commit e028238](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/e0282384bdf4c235c307df672c82a716130f96a1/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)). Two other packages were rejected: cedict-json has no header and has published nothing since 2025-12-14 ([cedict-json](https://registry.npmjs.org/cedict-json)), and cc-cedict downloads the newest file from mdbg.net while it is being installed, so its content is not reproducible ([cc-cedict 1.1.1](https://registry.npmjs.org/cc-cedict/-/cc-cedict-1.1.1.tgz)).

**How the app uses it** (from the code):

- Pinyin check: every HSK word, phrase and example is compared word by word with the CC-CEDICT readings ([scripts/lib/cedict.mjs](../scripts/lib/cedict.mjs), [scripts/validate.mjs](../scripts/validate.mjs)). A difference needs a recorded decision (section 1).
- Meanings: an HSK word's English meanings are the CC-CEDICT meanings of the same reading, with measure words split off, variant cross-references dropped where a real meaning exists, and surnames last ([scripts/import-hsk.mjs](../scripts/import-hsk.mjs)). The pinyin the app displays still comes from the HSK lists, apart from three apparent misprints corrected by a recorded decision ([data/hsk/resolutions.json](../data/hsk/resolutions.json)).
- Offline checks: a committed subset of 16,118 entries that keeps the release header and holds every HSK word, its characters and every dictionary word inside the app's Chinese texts ([data/sources/cedict-subset.json](../data/sources/cedict-subset.json), [scripts/lib/cedict-subset.mjs](../scripts/lib/cedict-subset.mjs)) and a list of simplified and traditional-only characters ([data/sources/charset.json](../data/sources/charset.json)) let the validator run without the full file.
- Audio: a word recording is used only when CC-CEDICT has exactly one reading for those characters (2.3; [scripts/import-hsk.mjs](../scripts/import-hsk.mjs)), and the speech-generation script forces the reading where it has more than one ([scripts/generate-tts.mjs](../scripts/generate-tts.mjs)).
- Display: cross-references inside meanings are shown with tone marks ([scripts/build-app-data.mjs](../scripts/build-app-data.mjs)).

**Decision.** CC-CEDICT stays the reference dictionary, pinned to release 2026-05-18 for v1. It may be redistributed under an open licence, its readings match the HSK 3.0 (2021) list for 98.2% of words, and its plain format is easy to check ([release file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt), [ivankra/hsk30](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ivankra/hsk30/master/hsk30.csv)).

**Left for later.**

- Move to release 2026-10-31 or newer from the daily mirror pinned to a commit ([release of 2026-10-31](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/55a0cf1226b044c59579dd8e0a32114ca6a14a11/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt); the same commit is on the [Go module proxy](https://proxy.golang.org/github.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/@v/v0.0.0-20261101153544-55a0cf1226b0.zip)), re-import and review the changed entries the app uses. When fetching from MDBG or any "latest" copy, add the checks the research proposes: the licence line equals the CC BY-SA 4.0 link, the number of entries equals `#! entries`, and neither the date nor the entry count falls below the release in use ([mirror log](https://github.com/rhcarvalho/cedict/tree/55a0cf1226b044c59579dd8e0a32114ca6a14a11)). The current pin cannot change silently (the npm version is fixed and its checksum is checked), so the stale-file problem does not affect it today.
- Complete the credit in the data files. The About page now names CC-CEDICT, MDBG with a link to its page, the licence, the release, Paul Andrew Denisowski's CEDICT copyright notice and what was changed ([app/src/views/about.js](../app/src/views/about.js)). The canonical word files name the release, licence and page ([data/hsk/words-\*.json](../data/hsk/)); the app's word files carry no notice, and only [app/data/manifest.json](../app/data/manifest.json) names the source.

### 2.2 Example sentences

**Tatoeba licence and credit.** Tatoeba licenses each sentence separately: CC BY 2.0 France by default, some sentences CC0 1.0 (public domain) ([Tatoeba licence code](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/src/Lib/Licenses.php), [downloads page](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/templates/Pages/downloads.php)). Its Terms of Use allow using, changing and distributing a sentence "only if the name of the author is cited" ([Terms of Use §6.2](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/resources/locales/en/terms-of-use.html)), and the licence also asks for its link and the notices to be kept ([CC BY 2.0 FR legal code, art. 4](https://github.com/creativecommons/cc-legal-tools-data/blob/a0dac18d1a773b4cdb7d8dd5d9bf4efab7901030/docs/licenses/by/2.0/fr/legalcode.fr.html)). The author is the username of the sentence's owner; owners can change, and "orphan" sentences have none ([Tatoeba sentence controller](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/src/Controller/SentencesController.php)). The Chinese sentence and its English translation can have different owners and licences, so both need a credit.

Tatoeba prescribes no credit format. The research proposes "Tatoeba #*id* by *owner*, CC BY 2.0 FR" for each sentence, with a link to `https://tatoeba.org/sentences/show/<id>`. This matches the line-by-line credit that manythings.org uses, for example "CC-BY 2.0 (France) Attribution: tatoeba.org #538123 (CM) & #891077 (Martha)" ([manythings extract](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/312527814/python-practice/9a3c3cf2dc61442924b7a9ee2b387c9dc961fb06/nlp/04translation-seq2seq/data/raw/cmn.txt)).

**Tatoeba audio is not usable.** Recordings carry their contributor's licence, not the sentence's. The possible values are "no licence for use outside Tatoeba", CC0 1.0, CC BY 4.0, CC BY-NC 4.0, CC BY-SA 4.0 and CC BY-NC-ND 3.0 ([licence code](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/src/Lib/Licenses.php)). When licences were assigned in 2016, 23 of 29 contributors got CC BY-NC 4.0 (no commercial use), 2 got CC BY-NC-ND 3.0, 3 CC BY 4.0 and 1 CC BY-SA 4.0 ([2016 data update](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/docs/database/updates/2016-12-22_2-data.sql)). With licences set per contributor and most of the 2016 ones forbidding commercial use, the research rules out Tatoeba audio. **UNVERIFIED**: a third-party count of the 2026-09-19 export found only one CC0 Mandarin sentence, and 5,825 Mandarin recordings of which 5,741 have no licence and 84 are CC BY-NC 4.0 ([hanzitutor research note](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hherb/hanzitutor/fb0fda6949371b02f9579379a32f60a04a0d7f16/docs/research/SENTENCE_CORPORA_LICENCES.md)). If that holds, practically every Mandarin sentence needs a credit, and no Mandarin recording may be reused outside Tatoeba apart from 84 that allow non-commercial use only.

**Reachability.** The export server and the API answered HTTP 403 from the research environment ([exports](https://downloads.tatoeba.org/exports/), [API probe](https://api.tatoeba.org/v1/sentences/1)). What is known about them comes from Tatoeba's own source code. There are weekly exports with per-language files, one of them listing each sentence's owner; the downloads page says they appear every Saturday at 6:30 UTC, while a comment in the export script says 9 AM (the page is the public statement and is used here) ([downloads page](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/templates/Pages/downloads.php), [export SQL](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/docs/database/scripts/weekly_exports.sql)). There is also an API with filters for native-speaker owners, script (simplified or traditional) and unapproved sentences ([API code](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/src/Controller/VHosts/Api/SentencesController.php)). The copies that could be reached fall short:

- An npm package with the Mandarin-English pairs of 2026-05-20 (76,607 pairs) keeps the sentence ids but not the owners or licences, and labels the data "MIT", which misstates it; Tatoeba's licences govern ([npm package](https://registry.npmjs.org/tatoeba-sentence-pairs-in-mandarin-chinese-english), [pairs file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/tatoeba-sentence-pairs-in-mandarin-chinese-english/7df8b93466f009310de22273973b34de7a20bf62/data/Sentence%20pairs%20in%20Mandarin%20Chinese-English%20-%202026-05-20.tsv)).
- manythings.org's cmn-eng.zip keeps both ids and both owners on every line (the format was checked on a copy), but the current file's date and size are **UNVERIFIED** ([manythings extract](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/312527814/python-practice/9a3c3cf2dc61442924b7a9ee2b387c9dc961fb06/nlp/04translation-seq2seq/data/raw/cmn.txt), [manythings file](https://www.manythings.org/anki/cmn-eng.zip)).
- The Helsinki Tatoeba-Challenge data has no ids, contradicts itself on the licence (CC BY 2.0 FR in the README, CC BY-NC-SA 4.0 in the licence file) and mixes in Cantonese and Wu ([Helsinki-NLP repository](https://github.com/Helsinki-NLP/Tatoeba-Challenge/tree/5509af44a74016748c26f41ab164b64f48445f3f)).

**Quality.** Of the 64,903 Mandarin sentences in the 2026-05-20 pairs, 46.7% are written in simplified characters, 45.1% in traditional, 7.9% could be either and 0.3% mix both; 1.7% contain Latin letters ([pairs file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/tatoeba-sentence-pairs-in-mandarin-chinese-english/7df8b93466f009310de22273973b34de7a20bf62/data/Sentence%20pairs%20in%20Mandarin%20Chinese-English%20-%202026-05-20.tsv)). The Terms of Use say translations are not checked by professionals and contributors' skills are not checked ([Terms of Use §5.1](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/resources/locales/en/terms-of-use.html)). Keeping short sentences (up to 12 characters) that are not traditional and have no Latin letters or digits leaves about 28,000; about 3,960 of them use only HSK 3.0 level-1 words and about 8,300 only level 1-2 words, before any native-speaker filter or human review ([pairs file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/tatoeba-sentence-pairs-in-mandarin-chinese-english/7df8b93466f009310de22273973b34de7a20bf62/data/Sentence%20pairs%20in%20Mandarin%20Chinese-English%20-%202026-05-20.tsv), [ivankra/hsk30](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ivankra/hsk30/master/hsk30.csv)).

**Alternatives.**

| Source | Licence | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- |
| no7z/hsk-sentences-audio: 4,354 sentences graded to HSK 3.0 levels 1-6, with synthetic audio | data CC BY-SA 4.0; who wrote and who reviewed the sentences is not documented ([npm package](https://registry.npmjs.org/hsk-sentences-audio), [attribution file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/no7z/hsk-sentences-audio/main/ATTRIBUTION.md)) | usable only after native review |
| Example sentences in the official GF 0025-2021 grammar volume | ©2021 Beijing Language and Culture University Press, a commercial book ([scanned copy](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%203/HSK%203-2021/2021-%E5%9B%BD%E9%99%85%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%95%99%E8%82%B2%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%B0%B4%E5%B9%B3%E7%AD%89%E7%BA%A7%E6%A0%87%E5%87%86%EF%BC%88%E7%AC%AC%E4%B8%89%E5%88%86%E5%86%8C%20%E8%AF%AD%E6%B3%95%EF%BC%89.pdf)) | reference for checking correctness only |
| Chinese Grammar Wiki (AllSet Learning) | CC BY-NC-SA 3.0, plus no use in any app that earns money from advertising; read from copies of the notice, the live site was blocked ([UD_Chinese-Beginner](https://github.com/UniversalDependencies/UD_Chinese-Beginner/tree/be33e2440b9b9a93cc90dcf371cbc63a59e6bd06), [ivankra/asg](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ivankra/asg/1d5731546972768c6063842f816acf99ce258dfa/README.md)) | human reference only |
| Universal Dependencies Chinese treebanks | Beginner: CC BY-NC-SA 3.0; GSD and GSDSimp: CC BY-SA 4.0, Wikipedia text without English; PUD: CC BY-SA 3.0, news and Wikipedia ([UD_Chinese-Beginner](https://github.com/UniversalDependencies/UD_Chinese-Beginner/tree/be33e2440b9b9a93cc90dcf371cbc63a59e6bd06), [UD_Chinese-GSDSimp](https://github.com/UniversalDependencies/UD_Chinese-GSDSimp/tree/4231dfd59866fa5999ad4a6bc1fdecd7985b3b59), [UD_Chinese-PUD](https://github.com/UniversalDependencies/UD_Chinese-PUD/tree/2849afd946a8c01b3e9acdf3e7afa8670cf2777d)) | not a fit: non-commercial or adult register |
| Wiktionary examples, Common Voice prompts, OPUS corpora, a Hugging Face Tatoeba-HSK set | **UNVERIFIED**: one secondary report only ([hanzitutor note](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hherb/hanzitutor/fb0fda6949371b02f9579379a32f60a04a0d7f16/docs/research/SENTENCE_CORPORA_LICENCES.md)) | not assessed |

**Decision for v1.** No outside sentence corpus. Tatoeba is the best candidate, but the owner and licence of each sentence, which the credit needs, could not be fetched from the build environment; the reachable copies lack them ([npm package](https://registry.npmjs.org/tatoeba-sentence-pairs-in-mandarin-chinese-english)); and since Tatoeba does not guarantee correctness ([Terms of Use §5.1](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/resources/locales/en/terms-of-use.html)), the research requires a native speaker to check every sentence before it is shown.

**What the app does today.** The "In a phrase" (cloze) card shows one of the app's own verified phrases with the word blanked out, chosen among the phrases the build links to that word by characters and reading, and a word gets this card only if it occurs inside such a phrase ([app/src/study-items.js](../app/src/study-items.js), [app/src/data.js](../app/src/data.js), [scripts/build-app-data.mjs](../scripts/build-app-data.mjs)). Those phrases live in [data/situations/](../data/situations/); only items with status "verified" reach the app, and every word in them is checked against CC-CEDICT ([scripts/lib/content.mjs](../scripts/lib/content.mjs), [scripts/validate.mjs](../scripts/validate.mjs)). No sentence from an unchecked corpus is shown. Some phrase records cite a Tatoeba sentence, by id and link, as evidence of how a phrase is used.

**Left for later.**

- A small curated Tatoeba set (hundreds, not thousands), fetched from a machine that can reach tatoeba.org, using the signals in Tatoeba's own data: native-speaker owners, simplified script, not unapproved or orphaned, no "@needs native check" tag, a direct English translation, short, within the learner's HSK level ([API code](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/src/Controller/VHosts/Api/SentencesController.php)). The learner's partner reviews each sentence; each is stored with id, owner, licence and export date in its own file under its original licence, and shown with its credit.
- Phrase source lists that quote a Tatoeba sentence give its id and link but not the contributor's name; add the name and the licence there to meet Tatoeba's terms ([Terms of Use §6.2](https://github.com/Tatoeba/tatoeba2/blob/f2c80b81032630648f7896182174a9f5784ccde2/resources/locales/en/terms-of-use.html)).

### 2.3 Audio

**What the learner hears today** ([app/data/words-\*.json](../app/data/), built 2026-11-02):

| Items | Count | Sound |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Single characters with tone 1-4 whose syllable file exists | 1,672 | the recording of that syllable by Chen Wang, named by its pinyin, so the reading is fixed |
| Words of two or more syllables whose characters have exactly one CC-CEDICT reading, equal to the app's | 5,711 | a word recording by Yue Tan |
| All other words | 4,983 | the device's Chinese voice, labelled "Device voice (synthetic)" |
| Phrases, kinship terms, and the correct grammar and pitfall examples | all | the device's voice, labelled ([app/src/views/parts.js](../app/src/views/parts.js), [app/src/views/reference.js](../app/src/views/reference.js)) |

| HSK level (2025 syllabus) | Words | With a recording | Device voice |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 300 | 265 (88%) | 35 |
| 2 | 198 | 161 (81%) | 37 |
| 3 | 497 | 440 (89%) | 57 |
| 4 | 997 | 813 (82%) | 184 |
| 5 | 1,591 | 1,328 (83%) | 263 |
| 6 | 1,792 | 1,335 (74%) | 457 |
| 7-9 | 5,583 | 2,630 (47%) | 2,953 |
| Older lists only | 1,408 | 411 (29%) | 997 |
| All | 12,366 | 7,383 (60%) | 4,983 |

For a learner at HSK 1-2 this means 72 of 498 words use the device voice today, among them very common ones: 的, 了 and 吗 (neutral-tone syllables, for which no usable recording exists), 你好, 中国, 手机 and 不客气 (no recording), and 东西 and 多少 (a recording exists, but CC-CEDICT lists more than one reading, so it cannot be trusted).

Of the 4,983 words on the device voice, 4,825 have no recording; 73 have one, but CC-CEDICT's reading differs from the app's (71 of them only by a neutral tone, such as 学生 xuéshēng against xue2 sheng5); 69 have one, but CC-CEDICT lists more than one reading; 15 are neutral-tone single characters; and 1 has no syllable file the app uses (嗯 ǹg) ([data/hsk/words-\*.json](../data/hsk/)). The six characters read jù (句, 聚, 据, 距, 剧, 锯) now get their recording: audio-cmn stores that syllable as jv4, and the word import applies this mapping, as the pinyin chart and the tone drills do ([scripts/import-hsk.mjs](../scripts/import-hsk.mjs), [scripts/build-app-data.mjs](../scripts/build-app-data.mjs), [app/src/lib/tonedrill.js](../app/src/lib/tonedrill.js)).

The tone trainer uses recordings only, never the device voice: 1,648 syllable files (412 syllables × 4 tones) and two-syllable word recordings pronounced with their dictionary tones ([app/src/views/tones.js](../app/src/views/tones.js), [app/data/tones.json](../app/data/tones.json)). All 7,359 audio files take 42.4 MB ([app/data/manifest.json](../app/data/manifest.json)).

**Why the device voice is only a labelled fallback.** Browser speech synthesis cannot be told which reading to use, and it differs from device to device:

- The Web Speech API lets a page send SSML, the markup that could carry a reading, but engines that do not support it must strip the tags and read the text; the voice list may be empty until the browser announces it ([Web Speech API draft](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebAudio/web-speech-api/main/index.bs)).
- Chrome on Android strips SSML before the text reaches the Android engine, offers one voice per locale (language and region) instead of a choice of voices, and cuts off a playing utterance when a new one starts ([Chromium tts_android.cc](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/content/browser/speech/tts_android.cc), [TtsPlatformImpl.java](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/content/public/android/java/src/org/chromium/content/browser/TtsPlatformImpl.java)). A practitioner report says it may fall back to an English voice when the Mandarin voice pack is not installed, and that Edge on Android lists no voices at all ([Readium](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/readium/speech/main/docs/WebSpeech.md)).
- On iPhone and iPad every browser uses WebKit (in the EU this could change under the Digital Markets Act; not checked). WebKit passes the text as a plain string, so there is no SSML; it exposes only system voices, loads the voice list asynchronously from OS 26.3 on, and speaks only after a tap ([WebKit PlatformSpeechSynthesizerCocoa.mm](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/main/Source/WebCore/platform/cocoa/PlatformSpeechSynthesizerCocoa.mm), [WebKit SpeechSynthesis.cpp](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/main/Source/WebCore/Modules/speech/SpeechSynthesis.cpp)). Voices the user downloads (enhanced or premium) are reported not to be available to web pages ([Readium](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/readium/speech/main/docs/WebSpeech.md), [mandarin-grove](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anandman/mandarin-grove/main/README.md)).
- **UNVERIFIED** voice names (one practitioner list): Apple's preloaded Mandarin voices are Tingting, Yu-shu and Li-Mu (rated low or normal quality) and Haohao (Shaanxi accent); Android's Google voices are cmn-CN-x-ccc, -ssa, -ccd and -cce ([Readium voice list](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/readium/speech/main/json/cmn.json)).
- Many characters have several readings (行 háng/xíng, 长 cháng/zhǎng, 觉 jué/jiào …): 367 of the 10,969 HSK 3.0 entries have more than one CC-CEDICT reading, 280 of them single characters (an upper bound: rare and surname readings count too), and a character spoken on its own gives the engine no context ([complete-hsk-vocabulary](https://github.com/drkameleon/complete-hsk-vocabulary), [CC-CEDICT via npm](https://registry.npmjs.org/@leonsilicon/cc-cedict)). Even with a whole sentence, open-source tools chose the right reading of such characters only 78.6% to 97.9% of the time on the CPP benchmark ([g2pM](https://pypi.org/pypi/g2pM/json)).
- **UNVERIFIED**: offline use and consistency. WebKit marks every voice as on-device, but on Android it depends on which voice data is installed, so the learner may hear different voices, and different readings, on different devices ([WebKit](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/main/Source/WebCore/platform/cocoa/PlatformSpeechSynthesizerCocoa.mm), [Readium](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/readium/speech/main/docs/WebSpeech.md)). Whether an iPhone home-screen app behaves differently from a Safari tab is also **UNVERIFIED**; no source documents a difference, so it needs a device test ([WebKit SpeechSynthesis.cpp](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/main/Source/WebCore/Modules/speech/SpeechSynthesis.cpp)).

The app picks a mainland Mandarin (zh-CN) voice first and never a Cantonese one, lets the learner choose and test a voice in Settings, and names the sound source next to the play buttons ([app/src/audio.js](../app/src/audio.js), [app/src/views/settings.js](../app/src/views/settings.js), [app/src/views/parts.js](../app/src/views/parts.js)).

**Cloud text-to-speech.** Generating the audio once, in a build script, solves both problems: the reading can be forced, and every device plays the same file.

| | Google Cloud Text-to-Speech | Microsoft Azure AI Speech | Amazon Polly |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Mandarin voices | Standard and WaveNet (cmn-CN) since 2019; Chirp 3: HD and Gemini-TTS also list cmn-CN ([Google blog](https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/cloud-text-to-speech-expands-its-number-of-voices-now-covering-33-languages-and-variants), [Google samples](https://github.com/GoogleCloudPlatform/generative-ai/tree/main/audio/speech/getting-started)) | many zh-CN neural voices, e.g. Xiaoxiao, Yunxi, Yunjian (2024 documentation) ([Azure voice list](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/85d04530858a8108d3258c21eea82724f062542e/articles/ai-services/speech-service/includes/language-support/tts.md)) | one, Zhiyu (female) ([Polly guide](https://github.com/awsdocs/amazon-polly-developer-guide)) |
| Forcing a reading | pinyin with tone numbers, neutral tone 5: `<phoneme alphabet="pinyin" ph="chao2 yang2">朝阳</phoneme>` ([API definition](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/googleapis/googleapis/master/google/cloud/texttospeech/v1/cloud_tts.proto), [phonemes page](https://docs.cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/docs/phonemes)) | a pinyin-based phone set: "ba 1", ü written v ([Azure zh-CN phone set](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/85d04530858a8108d3258c21eea82724f062542e/articles/ai-services/speech-service/includes/phonetic-sets/text-to-speech/zh-cn.md)) | `<phoneme alphabet="x-amazon-pinyin" ph="bo2">薄</phoneme>` ([Polly guide](https://github.com/awsdocs/amazon-polly-developer-guide)) |
| Price per million characters | WaveNet and Standard US\$4, first 4 million free each month; Neural2 US\$16 and Chirp 3: HD US\$30, first 1 million free; Gemini-TTS billed per token, no free tier ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing)) | **UNVERIFIED** | Standard US\$4, Neural US\$16, Generative US\$30 (price list of 2026-10-22); free tier not verified ([AWS price list](https://pricing.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/offers/v1.0/aws/AmazonPolly/current/index.json)) |
| What is counted | SSML tags count (except `<mark>`); at most 5,000 bytes per request ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing), [API definition](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/googleapis/googleapis/master/google/cloud/texttospeech/v1/cloud_tts.proto)) | each Chinese character counts twice; phoneme markup counts; the free tier allows 20 requests a minute ([Azure TTS overview](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/85d04530858a8108d3258c21eea82724f062542e/articles/ai-services/speech-service/text-to-speech.md), [Azure quotas](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/85d04530858a8108d3258c21eea82724f062542e/articles/ai-services/speech-service/speech-services-quotas-and-limits.md)) | SSML tags do not count ([Polly guide](https://github.com/awsdocs/amazon-polly-developer-guide)) |
| Keeping and shipping the audio | the output is Customer Data that the customer owns; no caching limit for TTS; no attribution requirement found; the output may not be used to build a competing service or model ([Google Cloud Terms](https://cloud.google.com/terms), [Service Specific Terms](https://cloud.google.com/terms/service-terms)) | **UNVERIFIED** | "cache and replay … at no additional cost", from an old developer-guide page; the AWS Service Terms were not read ([Polly guide](https://github.com/awsdocs/amazon-polly-developer-guide)) |

Points to know:

- Google's own pages disagree on the free allowance for WaveNet: the product page says the first 1 million characters a month, the pricing table 4 million (WaveNet now shares the Standard voices' billing code). The pricing table is the more specific source and is used here ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing), [product page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech)).
- Google lists WaveNet and Standard as "Legacy TTS models" but still sells them; Chirp 3: HD is described as powered by large language models ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing)). Google's terms bar generative AI services from apps likely to be used by people under 18. Whether Chirp 3: HD counts as one is not stated; WaveNet avoids the question, and for an adult learner neither is blocked ([Service Specific Terms](https://cloud.google.com/terms/service-terms), [Services Summary](https://cloud.google.com/terms/services)).
- Google's pages also disagree on whether Chirp 3: HD accepts SSML: a release note of 17 October 2025 says it does, including `<phoneme>`; the voices page says it does not ([release notes](https://docs.cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/docs/release-notes), [voices page](https://docs.cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/docs/list-voices-and-types)). Pinyin is defined as a pronunciation format in Google's API, and the Python client added pinyin for cmn-cn in version 2.27.0, while a comment in the same API still says custom pronunciations support only US English; this is unresolved. The SSML phonemes page could not be re-read during verification, so a listening test is required before relying on any of these routes ([API definition](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/googleapis/googleapis/master/google/cloud/texttospeech/v1/cloud_tts.proto), [Python client changelog](https://docs.cloud.google.com/python/docs/reference/texttospeech/latest/changelog)).
- **UNVERIFIED**: whether the engines apply tone changes (sandhi) to pinyin given in a phoneme tag. Azure's own example writes the spoken tones (一会儿 "yi 2 - hui r 4"), which suggests the string is read exactly as given ([Azure zh-CN phone set](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/MicrosoftDocs/azure-docs/85d04530858a8108d3258c21eea82724f062542e/articles/ai-services/speech-service/includes/phonetic-sets/text-to-speech/zh-cn.md)). The safe rule is to tag only items that need a forced reading and send everything else as plain text.
- **UNVERIFIED** other options: Kokoro-82M, an open-weight model under Apache-2.0 that could run locally ([kokoro on PyPI](https://pypi.org/pypi/kokoro/json)); edge-tts, which uses Microsoft Edge's online voices without any licence found for redistributing the output, so it is not suitable for a published app ([edge-tts on PyPI](https://pypi.org/pypi/edge-tts/json)).

**Open recordings: audio-cmn.** audio-cmn collects Mandarin recordings for web and mobile apps: syllables by Chen Wang, and HSK words by Yue Tan from the Shtooka project's cmn-caen-tan pack ([audio-cmn README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn/master/README.md)). At the pinned commit it has 1,707 syllable files and 8,596 word files ([audio-cmn repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn)).

- Syllables: 422 syllables × 4 tones (415 standard syllables and 7 interjections), plus 19 neutral-tone files. Names use tone numbers (cmn-ma3.mp3); ü is written v after l and n (lv4), and jù is stored as jv4 instead of ju4. Each file is about one second of 64 kbps MP3, recorded in 2013 by Chen Wang, a woman born in 1972, from Jiangsu ([repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn)).
- The neutral-tone files are unusable. The README says they were deleted, yet 19 remain (a5 to bing5). They last 0.84 to 5.09 seconds with continuous sound (ba5: 4.99 seconds without a pause) instead of about one second, so there are no usable neutral-tone syllable recordings ([repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn)). The verifier corrected the researcher's duration range (1.1-5.0 seconds); the conclusion did not change.
- Words: one file per character string, with no pinyin and no tags, so a single character's file cannot show which reading it holds; 338 of the 1,734 single-character files have more than one CC-CEDICT reading. A rough pitch check suggests 觉 is read jué and 数 shǔ, so reusing them for 睡觉 (jiào) or 数 "number" (shù) would be wrong ([repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn), [CC-CEDICT via npm](https://registry.npmjs.org/@leonsilicon/cc-cedict)).
- Coverage: 62.9% of HSK 3.0 (6,895 of 10,969 words) and 89.8% of HSK 2.0; even 北京, 中国, 不客气, 出租车 and 洗手间 are missing ([repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn), [complete-hsk-vocabulary](https://github.com/drkameleon/complete-hsk-vocabulary)).
- Licence of the syllables: CC BY-SA 3.0, embedded in all but four files together with "Copyright© 2013 Wang Chen, Lopez Hugo, Vion Nicolas". Unmodified clips bundled with other material do not put the app's code under that licence; trimmed or otherwise edited clips would have to be shared under CC BY-SA ([repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn), [CC BY-SA 3.0 text](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/spdx/license-list-data/main/text/CC-BY-SA-3.0.txt)). Four files repaired in October 2026 lost their embedded tags, so the credit cannot rely on the tags.
- Licence of the words: **UNVERIFIED**. The README says "CC-by-sa" without a version; the upstream Shtooka readme could not be reached; and the Wikimedia Commons page on Shtooka says its collections are "usually CC-BY", which may conflict. Until the readme is read, the safer assumption is CC BY-SA of unknown version, credited to Yue Tan and the Shtooka project ([README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn/master/README.md), [Commons:Shtooka](https://download.osmand.net/wiki/Commons:Shtooka)).

Other open sets, all **UNVERIFIED**: Tone Perfect from Michigan State University (410 syllables × 4 tones × 6 speakers; one third-party notice reports CC BY-NC 4.0, which rules out commercial use; the full set is given out by request form) ([MSU repository](https://d.lib.msu.edu/tone), [citing page](https://tone.lib.msu.edu/cite), [third-party licence notice](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anandman/mandarin-grove/main/audio-sources/LICENSE-MSU.txt)); Lingua Libre and Wikimedia Commons recordings (licences per file; not checked, the sites were blocked) ([Commons:Shtooka](https://download.osmand.net/wiki/Commons:Shtooka)); Taiwan's CNS11643 recordings (Taiwan Mandarin) and the Chinese Academy of Social Sciences syllable corpus (reuse limits) ([mandarin-grove audio notes](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anandman/mandarin-grove/main/docs/AUDIO.md)).

**Decision for v1.** Native recordings only where the file name fixes the reading; the device's voice, always labelled, for everything else; recordings only in the tone trainer. Correctness comes first: a recording named by its characters cannot prove its reading, and the device voice cannot be told one, so the app plays a human recording only when the reading is certain and says when a synthetic voice is speaking.

This departs from the research recommendation in two ways. The research recommended generating Google audio for every word and phrase, with the reading forced where needed, and audio-cmn syllables for the drills; it allowed the device voice only as a labelled fallback, and kept the audio-cmn word recordings for later, after a listening check and once their licence version is known.

1. The Google audio has not been generated. That needs a Google Cloud project with billing enabled and an API key ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing)), and the research makes a listening test of the pinyin control a precondition (see above). The script is ready.
2. The word recordings are used now, limited to words whose characters have only one reading in CC-CEDICT, equal to the app's ([scripts/import-hsk.mjs](../scripts/import-hsk.mjs)). No native listening check of them is recorded yet, and their licence version is **UNVERIFIED** (above).

**Upgrade path: [scripts/generate-tts.mjs](../scripts/generate-tts.mjs)** ([README, section "Audio"](../README.md#audio)).

- `node scripts/generate-tts.mjs --dry-run` counts clips, characters and cost; `GOOGLE_TTS_API_KEY=… node scripts/generate-tts.mjs` generates. The key is read only from the environment; behind a proxy add `NODE_USE_ENV_PROXY=1`.
- Single characters and words whose characters have more than one CC-CEDICT reading are sent as SSML, with the app's own pinyin in `<phoneme alphabet="pinyin">`; all other words and all phrases are sent as plain text, so the engine applies its normal tone changes. The default voice is cmn-CN-Wavenet-A at rate 0.9. Each file is named by a hash of text, pinyin, voice and settings, so a pinyin correction regenerates only that file, and an interrupted run can be resumed.
- Output is Google's MP3 at 32 kbps (4,000 bytes per second), which plays everywhere. Ogg Opus is documented for Android, Chrome and Firefox, and MDN notes that Safari plays Opus only in a CAF container (a note that may be dated) ([API definition](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/googleapis/googleapis/master/google/cloud/texttospeech/v1/cloud_tts.proto), [MDN codec guide](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/content/main/files/en-us/web/media/guides/formats/audio_codecs/index.md)).

Dry run for the data of 2026-11-02 ([scripts/generate-tts.mjs](../scripts/generate-tts.mjs)):

| Items without a recording | Clips | Billed characters |
| --- | --- | --- |
| HSK words | 4,983 (124 with forced pinyin) | 19,581 |
| Phrases | 621 | 5,335 |
| Kinship terms | 102 (8 with forced pinyin) | 731 |
| Grammar and pitfall examples | 188 | 1,240 |
| Total | 5,894 (132 with forced pinyin) | 26,887 |

That is US\$0 inside the monthly free tier (4 million characters for WaveNet, 1 million for Chirp 3: HD); at list price it would be about US\$0.11 with WaveNet or US\$0.81 with Chirp 3: HD ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing)). The phoneme tags are what costs characters: a tagged two-character word is billed as 68 characters instead of 2 ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing)). For comparison, the research's estimate for generating every word of HSK 2.0 and 3.0 (11,470) plus 400 phrases with targeted tags was 160,652 billed characters, US\$0.64 at the WaveNet list price ([pricing page](https://cloud.google.com/text-to-speech/pricing), [complete-hsk-vocabulary](https://github.com/drkameleon/complete-hsk-vocabulary)).

Size: about 5.3 KB per word clip at 32 kbps, taking the 1.32-second average of the human word recordings ([API definition](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/googleapis/googleapis/master/google/cloud/texttospeech/v1/cloud_tts.proto), [audio-cmn repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn)), so about 26 MB for the 4,983 words; phrases add a few MB (to be measured on the first batch). The deployment would then hold about 13,300 files, under the 15,000-file limit noted in the [README ("Deploy on Vercel")](../README.md#deploy-on-vercel).

**Left for later.**

1. A listening test before generating in bulk, with the learner's partner as listener: 20-30 items, including single polyphonic characters (行, 长, 了, 觉, 数), two third tones (你好, 可以), 一 and 不 (一个, 不是), erhua (哪儿, 一会儿) and ü syllables, which the script writes as v (lv4). How Google wants erhua and ü written inside a phoneme tag is not documented in the sources read ([API definition](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/googleapis/googleapis/master/google/cloud/texttospeech/v1/cloud_tts.proto)). Check the voice name with Google's ListVoices method first: individual cmn-CN voice names were not verified ([Google blog](https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/ai-machine-learning/cloud-text-to-speech-expands-its-number-of-voices-now-covering-33-languages-and-variants)). After generating, the partner checks the 132 forced-pinyin clips and a sample of the rest.
2. Connecting the output to the app is done for words, phrases, kinship terms and the correct grammar and pitfall examples: [scripts/build-app-data.mjs](../scripts/build-app-data.mjs) reads `data/audio/tts-index.json` and uses a generated clip wherever there is no recording and the clip's pinyin still matches, and the app plays it with its own label, "Generated voice (synthetic)" ([app/src/data.js](../app/src/data.js), [app/src/views/parts.js](../app/src/views/parts.js), [app/src/views/reference.js](../app/src/views/reference.js)).
3. Done: [scripts/import-hsk.mjs](../scripts/import-hsk.mjs) now applies the ju4 → jv4 mapping, as the drills do, so the six jù characters get their recording.
4. A native listening check of the 5,711 word recordings, and the licence version of Yue Tan's recordings from the Shtooka readme.
5. Done: the licence wording. The About page and the README now say that the recordings are shipped unmodified and keep their own licences ([app/src/views/about.js](../app/src/views/about.js), [README.md](../README.md)): CC BY-SA 3.0 for the syllables, with the copyright line of Wang Chen, Hugo Lopez and Nicolas Vion, and CC BY-SA of unknown version for the words ([CC BY-SA 3.0 text](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/spdx/license-list-data/main/text/CC-BY-SA-3.0.txt), [repository](https://github.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn)).
6. Optional: a second (male) voice; Azure once its price and terms are verified; Tone Perfect only if its licence fits.

### 2.4 Characters (stroke order)

**Options.**

- Hanzi Writer 3.7.3 (2025-09-17) animates the strokes of a character and can quiz stroke order. It is MIT licensed, has no dependencies and is 36,968 bytes minified (10,661 gzipped); it works with a plain script tag or as an ES module ([npm entry](https://registry.npmjs.org/hanzi-writer), [package](https://registry.npmjs.org/hanzi-writer/-/hanzi-writer-3.7.3.tgz)).
- Its data, hanzi-writer-data 2.0.1, has one JSON file per character (9,574 files), typically 3.3 KB, or about 1.5 KB gzipped ([data package](https://registry.npmjs.org/hanzi-writer-data/-/hanzi-writer-data-2.0.1.tgz)). The stroke shapes come from Make Me a Hanzi's graphics file, which is derived from Arphic fonts and licensed under the Arphic Public License. The `radStrokes` field (which strokes form the radical) is computed from Make Me a Hanzi's dictionary file, licensed LGPL-3.0 or later ([Make Me a Hanzi COPYING](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/skishore/makemeahanzi/master/COPYING), [hanzi-writer-data build script](https://github.com/chanind/hanzi-writer-data.git)). The package itself names only the Arphic licence; the research takes the cautious view (ship the LGPL text too, or remove the field), which is a legal judgement, not a settled fact.
- The Arphic Public License allows copying unmodified files if the licence text (ARPHICPL.TXT) goes with them. Changed files, and the licence counts converting the format as a change, need a notice saying how and when they were changed and must be freely available under the same licence. The app's own code is not affected ([Arphic Public License](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/skishore/makemeahanzi/master/APL/english/ARPHICPL.TXT)).
- By default Hanzi Writer fetches each character from cdn.jsdelivr.net. A custom loader can fetch the files from the app's own server instead, and then they also work offline; the app's security policy (`connect-src 'self'` in [app/vercel.json](../app/vercel.json)) requires this ([package](https://registry.npmjs.org/hanzi-writer/-/hanzi-writer-3.7.3.tgz), [MDN connect-src](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/content/main/files/en-us/web/http/reference/headers/content-security-policy/connect-src/index.md)).
- Size: the 899 distinct characters in the words of HSK 3.0 (2021) levels 1-3 need 2.15 MB, or 0.99 MB gzipped, and all 3,034 characters of the HSK lists in complete-hsk-vocabulary are covered ([data package](https://registry.npmjs.org/hanzi-writer-data/-/hanzi-writer-data-2.0.1.tgz), [complete-hsk-vocabulary](https://github.com/drkameleon/complete-hsk-vocabulary.git)). Checked against the app's own list on 2026-11-02: its 12,366 words use 3,127 characters, all covered except the numeral sign 〇, and the 371 characters of HSK levels 1-2 (2025) need 0.84 MB, or 0.39 MB gzipped ([data package](https://registry.npmjs.org/hanzi-writer-data/-/hanzi-writer-data-2.0.1.tgz)).
- Alternatives: animCJK draws each character as an SVG with its own animation and needs no JavaScript (8,360 simplified characters, including the 3,088 of the HSK 2025 list), but its files are about 1.8-1.9 times larger, it has no quiz, and its SVGs carry a style block that the app's strict style policy would block if placed in the page ([animCJK licence](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/parsimonhi/animCJK/master/licenses/COPYING.txt), [animCJK README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/parsimonhi/animCJK/master/README.md), [MDN style-src](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/content/main/files/en-us/web/http/reference/headers/content-security-policy/style-src/index.md)). cnchar-draw bundles the older Hanzi Writer 2.2.2 and loads its data from an unpinned CDN address ([cnchar-draw](https://registry.npmjs.org/cnchar-draw/-/cnchar-draw-3.2.6.tgz)).

**Decision.** No stroke order in v1. The learner's goals are vocabulary and speaking; stroke data adds offline storage and licence handling (Arphic plus LGPL) for little benefit to those goals ([data package](https://registry.npmjs.org/hanzi-writer-data/-/hanzi-writer-data-2.0.1.tgz), [Arphic Public License](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/skishore/makemeahanzi/master/APL/english/ARPHICPL.TXT)).

**What the app does today.** The "Characters" card shows the characters and asks for their pinyin and meaning; there is no stroke animation and no handwriting ([app/src/views/study.js](../app/src/views/study.js)).

**Left for later (v1.x).** An optional "Show stroke order" panel: host Hanzi Writer on the app's own server; copy only the needed data files, unchanged, with ARPHICPL.TXT and the LGPL and GPL texts (or remove `radStrokes` and add the change notices); load them with a loader that checks the server's answer; cache each character when it is first viewed; test under the final security policy in Safari and Chrome; possibly name the files by code point instead of by the character itself (untested). First ask whether the learner wants handwriting practice (the quiz mode) or only to watch the stroke order.

### 2.5 Word frequency

**wordfreq.** Version 3.1.1 (2023-11-21) is the latest release on PyPI; its code is Apache-2.0 and its data CC BY-SA 4.0. The Chinese list combines seven sources: Wikipedia, film and TV subtitles (OpenSubtitles and SUBTLEX-CH), news, Google Books, web text, Twitter, and the word list of the jieba segmenter, "whose provenance we don't really know" ([PyPI entry](https://pypi.org/pypi/wordfreq/json), [wheel](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/24/61/62835c475d69872d30689f284497853fe33fe1d6dd18f57346d13305861d/wordfreq-3.1.1-py3-none-any.whl)). The "large" Chinese list holds 334,609 words with Zipf values from 1.01 to 7.79 (的 7.79, 谢谢 5.16, 火车站 4.33). Keys are simplified characters only, and values are rounded to 0.01, so ties are common ([wheel](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/24/61/62835c475d69872d30689f284497853fe33fe1d6dd18f57346d13305861d/wordfreq-3.1.1-py3-none-any.whl)).

**Sunset.** In 2024 the author announced that the frequencies are "a snapshot of language usage through about 2021" and that "the data is unlikely to be updated again": text published since then is polluted by generated text, Twitter and Reddit data are no longer freely available, and she does not want to work on anything that could benefit generative AI ([README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rspeer/wordfreq/master/README.md), [SUNSET.md](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rspeer/wordfreq/master/SUNSET.md)). The data files have not changed since 2021-03-30 ([repository](https://github.com/rspeer/wordfreq.git)). A 2021 snapshot is fine for ordering HSK words; it matters only for very new vocabulary.

**Obligations.** Attribution under CC BY-SA 4.0, crediting the author as "Robyn Speer" (her notice file calls crediting her under another name a serious violation); credit the SUBTLEX authors and keep it clear that SUBTLEX is freely available data; attribution to OpenSubtitles; and an acknowledgement of Google Books Ngram "would be appreciated" ([wheel](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/24/61/62835c475d69872d30689f284497853fe33fe1d6dd18f57346d13305861d/wordfreq-3.1.1-py3-none-any.whl), [README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rspeer/wordfreq/master/README.md), [repository NOTICE.md](https://github.com/rspeer/wordfreq.git)). Since 2024 the README also answers "Can I convert wordfreq to … a CSV file?" with "No", because a CSV file has no room for the credits ([README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rspeer/wordfreq/master/README.md)). The licence itself allows any format as long as the credits are given; the app treats the author's position as a rule, so derived numbers travel with their credits and are never published as a bare list.

**Coverage.** The large list contains 497 of the 506 HSK 3.0 (2021) level-1 words (98.2%) and over 99% of levels 2-6. The misses are phrases that the segmenter splits (不客气, 请坐, 有一些) and erhua forms ([wheel](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/24/61/62835c475d69872d30689f284497853fe33fe1d6dd18f57346d13305861d/wordfreq-3.1.1-py3-none-any.whl), [complete-hsk-vocabulary](https://github.com/drkameleon/complete-hsk-vocabulary.git)). Written text often leaves out the erhua 儿, so erhua forms score lower than their base word (一点儿 3.95, 一点 5.51); but the 儿 in 女儿 (nǚ'ér) is a full syllable and must not be dropped ([wheel](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/24/61/62835c475d69872d30689f284497853fe33fe1d6dd18f57346d13305861d/wordfreq-3.1.1-py3-none-any.whl)).

**Alternatives considered.**

| List | Why not the main key |
| --- | --- |
| SUBTLEX-CH (Cai & Brysbaert 2010; 33.5 million words of film and TV subtitles) | the terms for using it directly are **UNVERIFIED**; it is already inside wordfreq, with Marc Brysbaert's permission for any use ([openlexicon SUBTLEX-CH page](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chrplr/openlexicon/master/datasets-info/SUBTLEX-CH/README-subtlex-ch.md), [wordfreq README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rspeer/wordfreq/master/README.md)) |
| BCC corpus list (Beijing Language and Culture University) | terms of use **UNVERIFIED** ([Pleco forum thread](https://www.plecoforums.com/threads/word-frequency-list-based-on-a-15-billion-character-corpus-bcc-blcu-chinese-corpus.5859/)) |
| jieba's dictionary counts | MIT, but the counts exist to drive word segmentation, and part of them comes from a 2006 Sogou word list ([jieba on PyPI](https://pypi.org/pypi/jieba/json), [jieba README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/fxsjy/jieba/master/README.md), [jieba Changelog](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/fxsjy/jieba/master/Changelog)) |
| complete-hsk-vocabulary "frequency" field | an undocumented blend of SUBTLEX-CH and an apparently BCC-derived list, with 93 placeholder values of 1,000,000 and conflicting licence labels ([complete-hsk-vocabulary](https://github.com/drkameleon/complete-hsk-vocabulary.git), [krmanik lists](https://github.com/krmanik/HSK-3.0-words-list.git)) |
| FrequencyWords zh_cn (OpenSubtitles 2018) | clearly licensed (content CC BY-SA 4.0) and close to speech, but subtitles only; its ranking correlates only 0.71 with wordfreq's (经理 "manager" ranks 3,089th in subtitles). Usable as a second signal ([FrequencyWords README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hermitdave/FrequencyWords/master/README.md), [zh_cn list](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hermitdave/FrequencyWords/master/content/2018/zh_cn/zh_cn_full.txt)) |

**Decision.** wordfreq 3.1.1, "large" Chinese list, used at build time to order the words inside each HSK level. It has the clearest licence for redistribution (CC BY-SA 4.0, the same as CC-CEDICT), mixes spoken and written sources, and covers 98-99% of HSK words ([wheel](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/24/61/62835c475d69872d30689f284497853fe33fe1d6dd18f57346d13305861d/wordfreq-3.1.1-py3-none-any.whl)).

**What the app does today.** [scripts/fetch-sources.mjs](../scripts/fetch-sources.mjs) takes `large_zh.msgpack.gz` from the pinned wheel, and [scripts/lib/msgpack.mjs](../scripts/lib/msgpack.mjs) decodes it without running wordfreq. Each word is looked up exactly by its simplified form; if a word ending in 儿 is missing, the form without 儿 is used. Words are ordered inside each 2025 level by wordfreq rank, and ties and words not found are ordered by syllabus number ([scripts/import-hsk.mjs](../scripts/import-hsk.mjs)). 12,307 of the 12,366 words (99.5%) have a rank; at HSK 1 only 不客气 and 有的 have none. The canonical files keep the Zipf value, the rank and a source block naming version, list and licence ([data/hsk/words-\*.json](../data/hsk/)); the app's files keep the rank only.

**Left for later.**

- Erhua: when a word's reading ends in an erhua r, take the higher value of the forms with and without 儿, as the research recommends. Today the 儿 form's lower value is used whenever it exists, so words like 一点儿 come later than their use suggests ([wheel](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/24/61/62835c475d69872d30689f284497853fe33fe1d6dd18f57346d13305861d/wordfreq-3.1.1-py3-none-any.whl)).
- Credits: the About page ([app/src/views/about.js](../app/src/views/about.js)) names wordfreq 3.1.1, Robyn Speer, CC BY-SA 4.0 and the sources of its Chinese data, including SUBTLEX-CH by Cai and Brysbaert (as freely available data), OpenSubtitles and Google Books Ngrams with a link, but not wordfreq's requested citation ([README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/rspeer/wordfreq/master/README.md)); the app's word files carry the rank without a credit block.
- The research preferred shipping a rank within each level, not raw Zipf values; the app ships the overall wordfreq rank ([scripts/build-app-data.mjs](../scripts/build-app-data.mjs)). Optional: FrequencyWords as a tie-breaker that favours spoken words.

### 2.6 Pinyin tooling

**pinyin-pro.** The research rates pinyin-pro the strongest JavaScript library for pinyin: version 3.29.5 (2026-10-10), MIT, no dependencies. It turns characters into pinyin using word context for characters with several readings, segments text, and matches pinyin searches ([npm entry](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-pro), [package](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-pro/-/pinyin-pro-3.29.5.tgz)). For this app it has four problems ([package](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-pro/-/pinyin-pro-3.29.5.tgz)):

- it applies the 一 and 不 tone changes by default, while the app stores dictionary tones, so every call needs `toneSandhi: false`;
- with tone numbers it writes the neutral tone as 0, where CC-CEDICT and the HSK data use 5;
- its converter from tone numbers to tone marks mishandles CC-CEDICT's spellings: 5 stays a digit, "u:" becomes "ǔ:", 1,069 of the 1,092 capitalised syllables that start with a vowel come out wrong (Ao4 → Aò, An1 → An̄), and the separate "r5" is not joined ([package](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-pro/-/pinyin-pro-3.29.5.tgz), [CC-CEDICT via npm](https://registry.npmjs.org/@leonsilicon/cc-cedict));
- without a bundler, importing even the converter loads 19 modules and 534,576 bytes, mostly dictionaries; the single-file browser build is 142 KB gzipped.

Whether its output agrees with the HSK lists on neutral tones is **UNVERIFIED**: its data has 喜欢 xǐ huan, as CC-CEDICT does, but the library was not run during verification ([package](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-pro/-/pinyin-pro-3.29.5.tgz)). Other packages are weaker: hotoo's "pinyin" ships a 7.6 MB browser build ([npm entry](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin)); pinyin-utils and pinyin-split are small but also mishandle CC-CEDICT spellings ([npm entry](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-utils)); pinyin-tone 3.0.0, published under the "next" tag, documents "u:" support but was not tested ([npm entry](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-tone)).

How pinyin-pro was used: during the research, the phrase-checking tool printed pinyin-pro's reading (tone changes off, tone numbers) next to the CC-CEDICT verdict as a second opinion; CC-CEDICT decided. It is not a dependency of the project ([package.json](../package.json) lists none) and is not shipped.

**The app's own module, [app/src/lib/pinyin.js](../app/src/lib/pinyin.js).** One ES module of about 410 lines without dependencies, imported both by the app in the browser and by the build scripts, so both always agree on syllables, tone marks and search keys. It covers:

- Syllables: the inventory of every syllable used in CC-CEDICT 2026-05-18, plus the nasal interjections n and ng.
- Reading pinyin: tone marks, tone numbers and CC-CEDICT spellings (u:, v, r5, capitals). Pinyin written without spaces is split by the spelling rule that a syllable starting with a, o or e begins a new word or follows an apostrophe (Xī'ān).
- Display: the tone mark on a or e, otherwise on the o of ou, otherwise on the last vowel; syllables joined, apostrophes added, erhua attached (yīdiǎnr).
- Validation: [scripts/validate.mjs](../scripts/validate.mjs) uses it to reject invalid syllables, two marks in one syllable, a mark on the wrong letter, tone numbers, v or u: instead of ü, and stray combining marks.
- Search: a toneless key with ü written v (nǚ'ér → nver) and a toned key (nǐ hǎo ma → ni3hao3ma5), so the learner can type xihuan, xi3huan or xǐhuan, with or without spaces ([app/src/lib/search.js](../app/src/lib/search.js)). Input is normalised to composed characters and looked up in a table of marked vowels. This avoids the trap the verifier found in the method first planned: decomposing first splits ü into u plus a separate diaeresis, which is then not recognised ([Unicode character data](https://www.unicode.org/Public/15.1.0/ucd/UnicodeData.txt)).
- Tone-change hints: the data stays in dictionary tones (一 yī, 不 bù, 你好 nǐ hǎo), as CC-CEDICT writes them ([release file](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/leonsilicon/cc-cedict/f0ff1c9513db0affe634835fd31eb16d7112dc48/data/cedict_1_0_ts_utf-8_mdbg.txt)), and the app derives the spoken form for display: 不 becomes bú before a fourth tone; 一 becomes yí before a fourth tone and yì before the others, but not at the end, after 第 or another numeral, or before a numeral or 月, 号, 日; two third tones in a row become second + third. Longer runs of third tones get no hint, because the result depends on how the phrase is grouped. Some official lists print the 一/不 changes (the HSK website does in about 166 words), so on import `citationPinyin` turns them back into dictionary tones ([ivankra/hsk30](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ivankra/hsk30/master/hsk30.csv)).

**Decision and why.** The app uses its own module for display, search, validation and tone-change hints; pinyin-pro stays a research tool. The module handles CC-CEDICT's spellings exactly, where the libraries examined do not; it keeps a 142 KB library out of an app that has no build step ([pinyin-pro package](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-pro/-/pinyin-pro-3.29.5.tgz)); one shared implementation means the build and the app cannot disagree; and it is small enough to unit-test ([tests/pinyin.test.mjs](../tests/pinyin.test.mjs), [tests/search.test.mjs](../tests/search.test.mjs)).

**Left for later.** Search by initials (xh for 喜欢), which the research recommended, is not implemented. The research also proposed running pinyin-pro over the data as a build-time cross-check that flags differences for human review and never corrects automatically ([package](https://registry.npmjs.org/pinyin-pro/-/pinyin-pro-3.29.5.tgz)); the repository does not do this today.

## 3. The three speaking situations

This section covers what sits behind the app's *Speak* tab: the phrases, etiquette tips and practice dialogues for travelling in mainland China, for visiting your partner's family, and for working with Chinese colleagues and clients, plus the travel facts that change and the *Pitfalls* and *Grammar* pages. For each part it says where the content came from, how it was checked, what the app shows today and what it leaves out. Links point to the sources the research read. **UNVERIFIED** marks anything whose final status is not "verified"; the app never shows such an item as fact.

**In short.** The research collected 633 phrases and 136 etiquette tips in 24 scenes, plus 102 kinship terms with 19 rules, 18 grammar points, 24 pitfalls and 31 travel facts ([data/situations/](../data/situations/), [data/reference/](../data/reference/)). A second, independent agent checked every item and looked for at least one further source, and the pinyin of every phrase was compared word by word with the CC-CEDICT dictionary. The app shows 621 phrases, 131 tips and all kinship terms, grammar points, pitfalls and travel facts; 12 phrases and 5 tips are hidden and listed in [UNVERIFIED.md](UNVERIFIED.md). The 36 practice dialogues only arrange verified phrases; an AI assembled them and no person has reviewed them yet ([AI-CONTENT.md](AI-CONTENT.md)). The most important dated fact: visa-free entry for Dutch passports (30 days) runs until 24:00 Beijing time on 31 December 2026, and on 2 November 2026 no extension had been announced ([Chinese embassy in the Netherlands](https://nl.china-embassy.gov.cn/eng/ls/zytz/202511/t20251104_11746916.htm); details in 3.3).

**How far the checks went.** Most evidence comes from files downloaded and read locally: official exam papers, two dictionaries, two collections of dialogues typed by native speakers, the Tatoeba sentence collection and transcripts of CCTV's evening news. The project's shared web-search budget ran out during the research stage, so most researchers had no web access at all; each verifier then had about 12 searches per topic. Web pages themselves could not be opened from the research environment, so every web source (teaching sites, Chinese press, forums, government pages) is the search engine's summary of a result, and its source entry in the data says so. Two more limits: the 现代汉语词典 text used is a community transcription whose definitions are still mostly the 5th-edition (2005) text ([CNMan/XDHYCD7th](https://github.com/CNMan/XDHYCD7th/blob/1e38ce15528b2fc3fdee0b2934b0951fcac51cb9/XDHYCD7th.txt)), and the dialogue collections are typed service chats, which show current wording and frequency but not the register of face-to-face talk. Tips that rest mainly on informal web sources carry confidence "medium" or "low" in the data; this section says so where it matters.

### 3.1 Where the content comes from and how it was checked

**Main sources.** The counts say how many items cite each source; most items cite several.

| Source | What it gives | Cited by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Official HSK, YCT and BCT sample papers and syllabi, by the exam bodies 国家汉办 (Hanban, older papers) and 中外语言交流合作中心 (CLEC, current ones), copies in GitHub `docrealm/docs-hsk` | Real exchanges written by the exam makers, with listening scripts. The HSK 1-2 and YCT papers print pinyin with the characters ([new HSK 1 sample](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%203/HSK3-%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7/HSK%EF%BC%88%E4%B8%80%E7%BA%A7%EF%BC%89%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7.pdf), [YCT 3 sample](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%202/%E8%80%83%E7%BA%B2%E5%92%8C%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7/YCT%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7%EF%BC%88%E5%9B%BD%E5%AE%B6%E6%B1%89%E5%8A%9E%EF%BC%89/v5/Y31005.pdf)). The business test (BCT) adds workplace dialogues, letters and short etiquette texts ([BCT A](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%202/%E8%80%83%E7%BA%B2%E5%92%8C%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7/BCT%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7%EF%BC%88%E8%AF%AD%E8%A8%80%E5%90%88%E4%BD%9C%E4%B8%AD%E5%BF%83%EF%BC%89/BCTA.pdf), [BCT B](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%202/%E8%80%83%E7%BA%B2%E5%92%8C%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7/BCT%E6%A0%B7%E5%8D%B7%EF%BC%88%E8%AF%AD%E8%A8%80%E5%90%88%E4%BD%9C%E4%B8%AD%E5%BF%83%EF%BC%89/BCTB.pdf)); the 2025 syllabus gives word levels and speaking tasks ([2025 HSK syllabus](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%203/HSK%203-2025/%E6%96%B0%E7%89%88HSK%E8%80%83%E8%AF%95%E5%A4%A7%E7%BA%B2%EF%BC%88%E6%97%A0%E6%B0%B4%E5%8D%B0%E7%89%88%EF%BC%89.pdf)) | 203 of 259 travel phrases, 114 of 181 family phrases, 141 of 193 work phrases |
| 《现代汉语词典》 (Contemporary Chinese Dictionary), 7th edition, community transcription ([CNMan/XDHYCD7th](https://github.com/CNMan/XDHYCD7th/blob/1e38ce15528b2fc3fdee0b2934b0951fcac51cb9/XDHYCD7th.txt)) | Meanings and labels: 〈口〉 colloquial, 〈方〉 regional, 〈书〉 literary, 客套话 polite formula | 334 phrases, 85 tips, all 102 kinship terms |
| 新华 dictionary data ([pwxcoo/chinese-xinhua](https://github.com/pwxcoo/chinese-xinhua/blob/fe6d6c2e8baa82187f4c96bbe042e43f96c05666/data/ci.json)) | A second dictionary for words and idioms | 35 phrases, 54 kinship terms |
| [CrossWOZ](https://github.com/thu-coai/CrossWOZ/blob/master/data/crosswoz/train.json.zip) (Tsinghua University, 5,012 dialogues) and [RiSAWOZ](https://github.com/terryqj0107/RiSAWOZ/blob/master/RiSAWOZ-data/source-data.zip) (Soochow University, 11,200 dialogues), typed by native speakers | How often a wording is really used today | 174 phrases |
| [relationship.js](https://github.com/mumuy/relationship/blob/35c44427befb6fd59e16f583b5b92a680a7948db/src/module/data/main.js) (mumuy), a Chinese kinship-term calculator | Kinship terms with northern and Cantonese variants | 74 of 102 kinship terms |
| CCTV 《新闻联播》 transcripts 2022-2026 ([archive](https://github.com/DuckBurnIncense/xin-wen-lian-bo)) | Current festival wishes and holiday dates | 33 family phrases, 14 family tips |
| Learner corpora [YACLC](https://github.com/blcuicall/YACLC/blob/main/valid.jsonl) and [MuCGEC](https://github.com/HillZhang1999/MuCGEC/blob/main/data/MuCGEC/MuCGEC_dev.txt) | Real sentences by learners of Chinese, with corrections | 12 of 24 pitfalls, 9 of 18 grammar points |
| [CC-CEDICT](https://www.mdbg.net/chinese/dictionary?page=cc-cedict), release 2026-05-18 | The word-by-word pinyin check | every phrase, term and example |

Web sources found through search fill the gaps, mostly for etiquette: 121 of the 259 travel phrases, 91 of the 181 family phrases and 45 of the 193 work phrases cite one, and so do 34 of 50 travel tips, 41 of 53 family tips and 20 of 33 work tips ([data/situations/](../data/situations/)). The family tips lean on them most.

**How an item became "verified".**

1. *Research.* One agent per topic collected phrases and tips, each with the exact quotation it rests on (the `evidence` field of each source).
2. *Independent check.* A second agent that had not done the research went through every item. It re-ran the pinyin check, found each quotation again in the local copies, and looked for at least one source the researcher had not cited. Its verdict per item is *confirmed*, *corrected*, *unverifiable* or *refuted*. A correction replaces the old text, and the old values stay in the item's `verification.notes`. The verifiers also added items where the research had gaps.
3. *Import.* An item gets status "verified" only if the verdict is confirmed or corrected and the dictionary check passes on the day of import ([ingest-research.mjs](../scripts/ingest-research.mjs)). Other items stay in the data as "unverified" or "refuted": the build leaves them out of the app ([content.mjs](../scripts/lib/content.mjs)) and the validator lists them in [UNVERIFIED.md](UNVERIFIED.md).
4. *Dictionary check.* Each phrase, kinship term and grammar or pitfall example is split into CC-CEDICT words, and each word's pinyin is compared with the dictionary ([cedict.mjs](../scripts/lib/cedict.mjs)). The split that uses the fewest words wins; only between splits with equally many words does the closer fit to the given pinyin decide. So a word is never broken into single characters to excuse a wrong reading (银行 written "yínxíng" stays 银行 and fails), while an ambiguous boundary is read the way that fits: 我不会说中文 becomes 说 + 中文, whereas simple longest-match splitting reads 说中 (shuōzhòng, "to hit the nail on the head" in [CC-CEDICT](https://www.mdbg.net/chinese/dictionary?page=cc-cedict)) + 文 and wrongly fails a correct sentence. The validator, which GitHub Actions runs on every push, fails if any shown item fails this check ([validate.mjs](../scripts/validate.mjs), [README](../README.md)).
5. *Text for the learner.* The import removes remarks about the research process ("this session", "search-result summary", "was not verified" and similar) from the usage notes, regional notes and explanations the app shows ([ingest-research.mjs](../scripts/ingest-research.mjs)). The claims themselves are not changed, and the full notes stay in the data files.

**Results.** Verdicts per item, from the data ([data/situations/](../data/situations/), [data/reference/](../data/reference/)):

| | Items | Confirmed | Corrected | Unverifiable | Refuted |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Phrases in the 24 scenes | 633 | 521 | 100 | 10 | 2 |
| Kinship terms | 102 | 71 | 31 | 0 | 0 |
| Tips in the 24 scenes | 136 | 93 | 38 | 5 | 0 |
| Kinship rules | 19 | 17 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
| Grammar points | 18 | 8 | 10 | 0 | 0 |
| Pitfalls | 24 | 15 | 9 | 0 | 0 |

The second check changed things: it corrected 131 of the 735 phrases and kinship terms (18%) and 40 of the 155 tips and kinship rules (26%). Almost every correction (168 of 171) changed a usage note or explanation; 9 also fixed the pinyin, 8 the characters and 5 the meaning. The dictionary check ([cedict.mjs](../scripts/lib/cedict.mjs)), run again on 2 November 2026 for this section, passes for all 735 phrases and kinship terms: 723 match CC-CEDICT exactly, and 12 differ only in a neutral (toneless) syllable, for example 房间里 written fángjiān li where CC-CEDICT gives 里 its full tone lǐ, or 月饼 yuèbing against yuèbǐng. All 207 grammar and pitfall examples match exactly. So the 17 hidden items are hidden because of the verifier's verdict, not because of the dictionary check.

One example shows what the check catches. The research proposed 劳驾，去中国银行怎么走？ ("Excuse me, how do I get to the Bank of China?"). The verifier refuted it, so it is **UNVERIFIED** and hidden: 劳驾 is northern and rather old-fashioned, the 2025 syllabus puts it at levels 7-9 against level 1 for 请问 ([2025 HSK syllabus](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%203/HSK%203-2025/%E6%96%B0%E7%89%88HSK%E8%80%83%E8%AF%95%E5%A4%A7%E7%BA%B2%EF%BC%88%E6%97%A0%E6%B0%B4%E5%8D%B0%E7%89%88%EF%BC%89.pdf)), and in 236,674 utterances of the two native-speaker collections it occurs 5 times against 3,540 for 请问 ([CrossWOZ](https://github.com/thu-coai/CrossWOZ/blob/master/data/crosswoz/train.json.zip), [RiSAWOZ](https://github.com/terryqj0107/RiSAWOZ/blob/master/RiSAWOZ-data/source-data.zip)). A dictionary site says people in the south are more likely to say 请问 or 不好意思 ([HanyuGuide](https://hanyuguide.com/zh/dictionary/%E5%8A%B3%E9%A9%BE?lang=zh)). The app teaches 请问，去中国银行怎么走？ instead and explains 劳驾 as a word you may hear in Beijing.

**Decision.** The app shows an item only when an independent check confirmed or corrected it and its pinyin passes CC-CEDICT; everything else stays in the data, out of the app, with its reason in [UNVERIFIED.md](UNVERIFIED.md). At HSK 1-2 you cannot yet judge a phrase yourself, so a wrong phrase learnt by heart costs more than a missing one. In the app each phrase card shows who says it (*You say*, *You hear* or *Either*), the characters, the pinyin and, where tones change in speech, the spoken form, the English, audio, the HSK words it contains, the usage and regional notes and up to four sources ([scene.js](../app/src/views/scene.js), [content.mjs](../scripts/lib/content.mjs)); every scene can be switched on as a daily study deck ([speak.js](../app/src/views/speak.js)). Items the research rated low confidence carry a "less certain" chip, and medium ones a note under their sources, so a tip resting on forum posts no longer looks the same as one taken from an exam paper ([parts.js](../app/src/views/parts.js)); this section also names the weaker ones.

### 3.2 Travel

| Scene | Phrases shown / researched | Tips shown / researched | Dialogues |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Greetings and politeness basics | 26 / 26 | 8 / 8 | 2 |
| When you don't understand | 23 / 23 | 2 / 2 | 2 |
| Asking for help and directions | 22 / 23 | 5 / 5 | 2 |
| Taxi and ride-hailing | 32 / 33 | 7 / 7 | 2 |
| Trains: tickets, seats and the station | 28 / 29 | 2 / 3 | 1 |
| Hotel: check-in, problems and services | 31 / 31 | 4 / 5 | 2 |
| Restaurant: table, ordering, the bill | 27 / 27 | 5 / 5 | 2 |
| Allergies and dietary needs | 18 / 19 | 5 / 5 | 1 |
| Paying and shopping | 25 / 25 | 6 / 6 | 2 |
| Pharmacy, doctor and emergencies | 23 / 23 | 4 / 4 | 0 |
| **Total** | **255 / 259** | **48 / 50** | **16** |
