| 太…了 ('too' / 'so…!') and 有点儿 ('a bit', usually unwelcome) | 太…了 = too / so …!; 有点儿 + adjective = a bit (usually unwelcome); adjective + 一点儿 = a bit more. | [GF 0025-2021 grammar](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%203/HSK%203-2021/2021-%E5%9B%BD%E9%99%85%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%95%99%E8%82%B2%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%B0%B4%E5%B9%B3%E7%AD%89%E7%BA%A7%E6%A0%87%E5%87%86%EF%BC%88%E7%AC%AC%E4%B8%89%E5%88%86%E5%86%8C%20%E8%AF%AD%E6%B3%95%EF%BC%89.pdf), [现代汉语词典](https://github.com/CNMan/XDHYCD7th/blob/1e38ce15528b2fc3fdee0b2934b0951fcac51cb9/XDHYCD7th.txt) |
| Time words, dates and clock times (年 月 日/号, 星期, 点 分 半 刻 差) | Dates go from large to small (2027年2月6日); 号 in speech, 日 in writing; 两点, 两点二十五, 五点半, 一刻, 差. | [GF 0025-2021 grammar](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%203/HSK%203-2021/2021-%E5%9B%BD%E9%99%85%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%95%99%E8%82%B2%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%B0%B4%E5%B9%B3%E7%AD%89%E7%BA%A7%E6%A0%87%E5%87%86%EF%BC%88%E7%AC%AC%E4%B8%89%E5%88%86%E5%86%8C%20%E8%AF%AD%E6%B3%95%EF%BC%89.pdf), [CC-CEDICT](https://www.mdbg.net/chinese/dictionary?page=cc-cedict) |
| 把 sentences (basic): doing something to a specific thing | 把 + a specific object + verb + where it ends up or the result: 请把杂志递给我. | [GF 0025-2021 grammar](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk/blob/bdff23362be0ffb4020505b7333a4443bcaa9ec1/HSK%203/HSK%203-2021/2021-%E5%9B%BD%E9%99%85%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%95%99%E8%82%B2%E4%B8%AD%E6%96%87%E6%B0%B4%E5%B9%B3%E7%AD%89%E7%BA%A7%E6%A0%87%E5%87%86%EF%BC%88%E7%AC%AC%E4%B8%89%E5%88%86%E5%86%8C%20%E8%AF%AD%E6%B3%95%EF%BC%89.pdf), [现代汉语词典](https://github.com/CNMan/XDHYCD7th/blob/1e38ce15528b2fc3fdee0b2934b0951fcac51cb9/XDHYCD7th.txt) |

**Decision.** Pitfalls and grammar are short reference pages with checked examples, not a course; they cover the mistakes English and Dutch speakers make most at HSK 1-3. **Today** both pages show every item with its examples and sources ([pitfalls.js](../app/src/views/pitfalls.js), [grammar.js](../app/src/views/grammar.js)). Nothing is hidden.

### 3.10 What is hidden, and what is left for later

**Hidden: 17 items, all listed with their reasons in [UNVERIFIED.md](UNVERIFIED.md).**

| Group | Researched | Shown | Hidden (**UNVERIFIED**) |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Travel phrases | 259 | 255 | 4 (2 refuted) |
| Travel tips | 50 | 48 | 2 |
| Family phrases | 181 | 176 | 5 |
| Family tips | 53 | 52 | 1 |
| Work phrases | 193 | 190 | 3 |
| Work tips | 33 | 31 | 2 |
| Kinship terms and rules | 121 | 121 | 0 |
| Grammar points and pitfalls | 42 | 42 | 0 |
| Travel facts | 31 | 31 | 0 |
| Dialogues (assembled, not researched) | 36 | 36 | 0 |

**Left for later:**

1. A person reads the 36 dialogues and marks the natural ones as reviewed.
2. The visa-free end date (31 Dec 2026) is re-checked in November-December 2026. Done since: the three visa-free cards carry `valid_until` 2026-12-31 and the app marks them "out of date" after that day ([facts.js](../app/src/views/facts.js)); the registration card follows the law's "within 24 hours of moving in".
3. A native speaker reads the medium- and low-confidence tips and the phrases that were adapted or assembled from attested parts; the app could then also show each item's confidence.
4. Done: the hidden wordings were removed from the tips that quoted them (学业进步, 学习进步, 快快长大, 手气最佳, 我司, 顺颂商祺), except 拜个晚年, which the tips' own sources attest; the validator guards this ([scripts/validate.mjs](../scripts/validate.mjs)).
5. Your partner checks what the family actually says and does (the kinship words of their province, the usual red-envelope sum, which New Year customs they keep), so that the regional notes can be made specific.
6. The lines the research could not source (3.2 and 3.5) are looked for again, and the 2027 holiday arrangement is added once the State Council publishes it.

## 4. Learning method, scheduler and a realistic plan

This section covers how the app decides which cards to show you each day, what the research says about learning Mandarin words and tones, the design that follows from it, and how long the HSK word lists take at different paces. Links point to the sources the research read. **UNVERIFIED** marks findings that could not be confirmed; they are reported as open questions, not as facts.

**In short.** The app schedules reviews with FSRS-6, which predicts what you will remember far better than the older SM-2 algorithm ([srs-benchmark](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md), [srs-benchmark repository](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark.git)), but no source seen shows that it makes anyone learn faster ([Settles & Meeder 2016](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/duolingo/halflife-regression/master/settles.acl16.pdf)). The best-supported method in this research is ear training for tones with immediate feedback ([Wang et al. 1999](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/106/6/3649/915818/Training-American-listeners-to-perceive-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext)); most other popular advice could not be checked. Each word is practised as Listen, then Speak, then (optionally) Characters, then In a phrase ([settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js)). At the default 10 new words a day with Listen + Speak, the app's model ([simulate-plan.mjs](../scripts/simulate-plan.mjs)) expects about 17 minutes of answers a day after a month and 35 after a year, and all HSK 4 words met after 200 days, sooner for every word you already know.

How far the checks went. The scheduler findings (4.1 to 4.3) were re-checked against fresh downloads of the primary files: npm packages, the benchmark's code and result files, and Anki's documentation. For the learning-method findings (4.4), the web-search budget was used up when the checker ran, so it could re-fetch only four primary files: the [Duolingo spaced-repetition paper](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/duolingo/halflife-regression/master/settles.acl16.pdf) and its [repository README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/duolingo/halflife-regression/master/README.md), the [srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md) and the [audio-cmn README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn/master/README.md). The other method findings marked verified rest on the publisher pages and abstracts the research stage read. Of the 26 method findings, 12 are verified, 1 is disputed and 13 remain **UNVERIFIED**.

### 4.1 The scheduler: FSRS, not SM-2

A spaced-repetition scheduler decides when each card comes back. Two families were compared.

- **SM-2** was written by Piotr Wozniak for SuperMemo in 1987. Each card has an "easiness factor" that starts at 2.5; the first two intervals are 1 and 6 days, and each later interval is the previous one multiplied by that factor ([FSRS wiki](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/awesome-fsrs.wiki.git), [Expertium](https://github.com/Expertium/Expertium.github.io.git)). It produces intervals only and has no estimate of how likely you are to remember. Anki's older scheduler is a variant of it ([Anki documentation](https://github.com/ankitects/anki.git)).
- **FSRS** keeps two numbers per card (FSRS-7 adds a second, fast stability component; [ts-fsrs documentation](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/ts-fsrs.git)). In FSRS-6, the version the app runs, they are stability (the number of days after which the chance of recall has dropped to 90%) and difficulty (1 to 10); from them FSRS predicts the chance of recall at any moment ([FSRS wiki, "The Algorithm"](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/awesome-fsrs.wiki.git)). The next review is set for when that chance falls to a target you choose, the desired retention (4.3).

**The benchmark.** The open srs-benchmark tests these algorithms on the review histories of 10,000 Anki users (the anki-revlogs-10k dataset, about 727 million reviews). Each algorithm is fitted on a user's older reviews and tested on the newer ones. The main table covers 9,999 collections and 349,923,850 reviews and leaves out repeats on the same day ([srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md)). Two scores are used here:

- *Log loss*: how far the predicted chance of recall is from what actually happened (lower is better). It measures calibration.
- *AUC*: how well the algorithm ranks the cards you will forget above the ones you will remember (0.5 is a coin toss, higher is better).

| Algorithm | Log loss | AUC | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| FSRS-7, weights fitted to each user | 0.3401 | 0.7167 | newest FSRS (ts-fsrs 6.0.0) |
| FSRS-6, weights fitted to each user | 0.3460 | 0.7034 | the model the app runs |
| FSRS-7, default weights | 0.3620 | 0.7029 | |
| FSRS-6, default weights | 0.3661 | 0.6956 | the app's set-up; August 2025 run |
| AVG: every card gets the user's average recall rate | 0.3945 | 0.4997 | baseline that ignores timing |
| ACT-R | 0.4033 | 0.5225 | |
| HLR (Duolingo) | 0.4694 | 0.6369 | |
| Ebisu v2 | 0.4989 | 0.6051 | |
| Anki's SM-2 variant, default settings | 0.616 | 0.6133 | August 2025 run |
| SM-2 | 0.722 | 0.6026 | August 2025 run |

Rows without a run note come from the current README, whose tables were regenerated on 2026-08-30 ([srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md)). SM-2 is no longer in it, so the rows marked "August 2025 run" come from the README version of August 2025, the last that listed SM-2 (its rows were removed on 2025-08-22), on the same 9,999 collections. FSRS-6 with fitted weights scored 0.3456 in that run, practically the same as today ([srs-benchmark repository](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark.git)). Two of the August 2025 rows have since been re-run in the benchmark's result files, with slightly different results (Anki's SM-2 0.6258, FSRS-6 with default weights 0.3664) ([result files](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/4ddb9fd9d6d7888ea52fc721d5ab0e6dd7a5e877/result/)). The table keeps the published figures, because they come from the same run as SM-2; the differences change no conclusion.

What the numbers say:

- Both SM-2 variants do worse than the AVG baseline on log loss (not on AUC). SM-2 makes no recall prediction of its own, so the benchmark scores it by assuming a 90% chance of recall at the end of each interval; the scores measure how well SM-2's intervals work as "90% points" ([benchmark code](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark.git)).
- Learner by learner: with default weights, which is what a new learner and this app have, FSRS-6 has a lower log loss than SM-2 for 98.1% of the 9,999 collections and than Anki's SM-2 for 97.8%; for FSRS-7 the shares are 97.5% and 97.3%. With weights fitted to each user, FSRS-7 wins for 99.7% and 99.5%. The verifier computed these shares from the benchmark's per-collection result files; the README shows them only as charts ([result files](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/4ddb9fd9d6d7888ea52fc721d5ab0e6dd7a5e877/result/)).
- The default weights were fitted on the reviews of about 10,000 users, and "even with the default parameters, FSRS is better than SM-2" ([ABC of FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/awesome-fsrs.wiki.git)). Fitting the weights to one learner helps further: 0.3401 against 0.3620 for FSRS-7 ([srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md)).
- When same-day repeats are included (10,000 collections, 519,296,315 reviews), FSRS-7 scores 0.3206 and FSRS-6 0.3842, worse than the AVG baseline's 0.3816. Earlier versions were designed for whole days; FSRS-7 is the first designed for fractional days ([srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md)).
- SM-2 has practical problems too. Repeated failures permanently lower a card's ease, so it stays stuck on short intervals ("ease hell", which FSRS does not suffer from), and SM-2 has no retention setting: to change how much you remember, Anki users have to tune an "interval modifier" by formula ([Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md)). Anki says FSRS is "much better at scheduling cards that have been reviewed with a delay", for example after a break ([Anki documentation](https://github.com/ankitects/anki.git)).

**Better prediction, not proven faster learning.** The checker of the method research added an important caveat: the benchmark measures prediction. Of the algorithms in the table above, FSRS is the best calibrated. It is not the best predictor overall: neural networks trained across many users do better (RWKV-Instant: log loss 0.2773, AUC 0.8329), and two simple models do slightly better than FSRS-7 on log loss, a moving average (0.3369) and logistic regression (0.3393), while ranking cards less well (AUC 0.7001 and 0.7108 against 0.7167) ([srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md)). The benchmark is published by Open Spaced Repetition, the group that also publishes FSRS ([srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md), [ts-fsrs on npm](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs)).

More important, no source seen shows that FSRS, or any adaptive scheduler, makes people learn faster. The one large field test in the sources ran for six weeks with just under 1 million Duolingo students and compared Duolingo's adaptive model (HLR) with Leitner boxes. The share of students who came back the next day changed by +0.3% for any activity and +0.3% for lessons (neither significant) and by −7.3% for practice sessions (significant). On 12.9 million student–word records, HLR predicted recall more accurately (mean absolute error 0.128, against 0.235 for Leitner and 0.445 for Pimsleur's fixed intervals), but in ranking which words a learner would forget, all models were close to chance (AUC 0.51 to 0.54), with Leitner marginally ahead (0.542 against 0.538). The paper's headline 12% engagement gain compared two versions of HLR, not adaptive against fixed scheduling ([Settles & Meeder 2016](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/duolingo/halflife-regression/master/settles.acl16.pdf)). A study that may show real learning gains from personalised review (Lindsey and colleagues, 2014, Spanish vocabulary) was noted as a lead but not read (**UNVERIFIED**).

How many fewer reviews FSRS needs than SM-2 is disputed (**UNVERIFIED**). The FSRS wiki says 20 to 30% fewer for the same retention, "based on simulation results" it does not identify ([ABC of FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/awesome-fsrs.wiki.git)). In the FSRS authors' own published simulation (5 years, 10 new cards a day), FSRS at desired retention 0.84 matched the average knowledge of Anki's SM-2 (6,633 against 6,645 cards) with 83.5 instead of 93.7 reviews a day, about 11% fewer, and 24.9 instead of 26.0 minutes ([SSP-MMC-FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/SSP-MMC-FSRS.git)). This document promises neither figure; where one is needed, it uses the smaller, because that simulation's table and code were checked.

**Decision.** FSRS, never SM-2. With default weights, FSRS-6 is better calibrated than SM-2 for about 98% of the learners in the benchmark, it aims at a retention you choose, it copes better with breaks, and it has no "ease hell". The app makes no claim that it speeds up learning. Today every card is scheduled by FSRS-6 with default weights ([srs.js](../app/src/lib/srs.js)).

### 4.2 The library: ts-fsrs 5.4.2 (FSRS-6) now, FSRS-7 later

The app uses ts-fsrs 5.4.2, published on 2026-09-01 under the MIT licence with no runtime dependencies ([ts-fsrs on npm](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs)). Its `dist/index.mjs` is a single self-contained ES module without imports, 61,245 bytes (13,384 gzipped), that a page can load directly with `<script type="module">` ([ts-fsrs 5.4.2 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-5.4.2.tgz)). The app keeps a copy at [`app/src/vendor/ts-fsrs.mjs`](../app/src/vendor/ts-fsrs.mjs), unchanged except for a header and the removed source-map comment, with the licence text next to it and a credit under More → About; no build step is needed (section 6).

ts-fsrs 6.0.0, published on 2026-10-28, makes FSRS-7 the default model ([ts-fsrs on npm](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs), [ts-fsrs 6.0.0 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-6.0.0.tgz)). FSRS-7 has 34 weights instead of 21, works in fractional days and is more accurate: log loss 0.3401 against 0.3460 for reviews a day or more apart, and 0.3206 against 0.3842 when same-day reviews count. It beats FSRS-6 for 79.4% of collections, and for 96.05% when same-day reviews count ([srs-benchmark README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md), [result files](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/4ddb9fd9d6d7888ea52fc721d5ab0e6dd7a5e877/result/)).

The scheduler research recommended 6.0.0 with FSRS-7 and called 5.4.2 an acceptable fallback, "since the next-day accuracy gap is small". The app uses the fallback for now, for four reasons:

1. **6.0.0 is new.** It was five days old on 2026-11-02. FSRS-7's default weights changed as recently as 2026-08-30 ([srs-benchmark repository](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark.git)), and Anki (version 26.09.3, with the fsrs crate 6.6.2) still schedules with FSRS-6 ([Anki repository](https://github.com/ankitects/anki.git), [fsrs 6.6.2](https://static.crates.io/crates/fsrs/fsrs-6.6.2.crate)).
2. **6.0.0 is not one file.** Its ES modules import five bare module paths (the package itself and four subpaths) from its new dependency, srs-kit. A browser cannot resolve those without a bundler or an import map with all five entries (import maps need Safari/iOS 16.4 or later); the alternative is a 198,410-byte UMD file (40,064 gzipped) that sets a global variable ([ts-fsrs 6.0.0 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-6.0.0.tgz), [MDN compatibility data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz)). The app has no build step.
3. **It adds a licence.** srs-kit is BSD-3-Clause, so its notice would have to ship as well as ts-fsrs's MIT notice ([srs-kit 1.0.0](https://registry.npmjs.org/@open-spaced-repetition/srs-kit/-/srs-kit-1.0.0.tgz)).
4. **Switching needs the review history.** FSRS-6 memory states cannot be converted to FSRS-7; cards have to be rebuilt by replaying their reviews ([ts-fsrs documentation](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/ts-fsrs.git)). The app stores each card's current state with its first and last review times, but no log of individual reviews ([srs.js](../app/src/lib/srs.js)).

Browser support is not a reason to wait: 6.0.0 needs Safari/iOS 15.4+, Chrome 93+ or Firefox 92+ because it calls `Object.hasOwn` ([MDN compatibility data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz)), and the app's own code already calls that function ([srs.js](../app/src/lib/srs.js)). ts-fsrs 6 can also still run FSRS-6, so the library and the model can be upgraded separately ([ts-fsrs 6.0.0 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-6.0.0.tgz)).

How the app sets up FSRS today ([srs.js](../app/src/lib/srs.js), [study.js](../app/src/views/study.js)):

| Setting | In the app | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Model | FSRS-6 with its 21 default weights, not fitted to you | defaults work from the first day (4.1) |
| Desired retention | 0.90 | see 4.3 |
| Learning steps | 1 and 10 minutes; after a lapse, 10 minutes | the ts-fsrs defaults; Anki advises against steps of a day or longer with FSRS ([Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md), [ts-fsrs documentation](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/ts-fsrs.git)) |
| Fuzz | on (ts-fsrs default: off) | Anki always adds a little random variation to intervals so that cards learned on the same day do not keep coming due together ([Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md), [ts-fsrs 5.4.2 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-5.4.2.tgz)) |
| Longest interval | 36,500 days | ts-fsrs default ([ts-fsrs 5.4.2 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-5.4.2.tgz)) |
| Buttons | Again, Hard, Good, Easy | press Again whenever you did not remember; Hard counts as remembered. If you press Hard after forgetting, "all intervals will be unreasonably high". Using only Again and Good works and costs little accuracy (log loss 0.3509 against 0.3456) ([Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md), [Anki documentation](https://github.com/ankitects/anki.git), [srs-benchmark repository](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark.git)) |

Left for later:

- **A review log** (card, answer, time) so that the app can move to FSRS-7 and fit weights to you without losing history.
- **FSRS-7**, once 6.0 has settled and the log exists, tested on the oldest phone the app should support.
- **Weights fitted to you.** The optimizer exists as a WebAssembly package (@open-spaced-repetition/binding 0.6.0, MIT; the wasm build is about 1.58 MB unpacked), which ts-fsrs recommends running in a background worker ([binding](https://registry.npmjs.org/@open-spaced-repetition/binding), [wasm build](https://registry.npmjs.org/@open-spaced-repetition/binding-wasm32-wasi), [ts-fsrs documentation](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/ts-fsrs.git)). Anki names "less than a few hundred" reviews as a common reason for a poor fit and suggests re-fitting about once a month ([Anki documentation](https://github.com/ankitects/anki.git)).
- **The day boundary.** For FSRS-6, ts-fsrs counts days between UTC calendar dates, so in the Netherlands the scheduler's day changes at 01:00 in winter and 02:00 in summer; FSRS-7 uses the exact time elapsed ([ts-fsrs 5.4.2 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-5.4.2.tgz), [ts-fsrs documentation](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/ts-fsrs.git), [IANA time zones](https://www.iana.org/time-zones)). The app's own study day runs from 04:00 to 04:00 local time and shows every card due before it ends ([queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js)).

### 4.3 Desired retention

Desired retention is the chance that you remember a card at the moment it comes due. Anki puts it this way: "The default value of 90% will schedule cards so you have a 90% chance of remembering them when they come up for review again" ([Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md)). It is not the share of all your cards that you know on a given day, which is higher: about 94.7% at a target of 90% by Expertium's estimate for an older FSRS curve, and 94.35% by the research's own calculation for the FSRS-6 default curve ([Expertium](https://github.com/Expertium/Expertium.github.io.git), [ts-fsrs 6.0.0 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-6.0.0.tgz)).

The default is 0.90 in ts-fsrs and in Anki ([ts-fsrs 5.4.2 package](https://registry.npmjs.org/ts-fsrs/-/ts-fsrs-5.4.2.tgz), [Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md)). Anki calls it "a good balance of retention and workload", warns that "above 90% the workload increases very quickly, and above 97% the workload can be overwhelming", and recommends keeping it "lower than 97%" ([Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md)). The FSRS authors call 70% to 97% reasonable and say desired retention, not the weights, is the setting to adjust ([ABC of FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/awesome-fsrs.wiki.git)).

What it costs. The FSRS authors simulated a 10,000-card deck with 10 new cards a day for 5 years, assuming FSRS predicts perfectly, with no same-day reviews and no fuzz ([SSP-MMC-FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/SSP-MMC-FSRS.git)):

| Desired retention | Reviews a day | Minutes a day | Cards known on average |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0.80 | 70.4 | 22.6 | 6,454 |
| 0.85 | 87.6 | 25.6 | 6,676 |
| 0.90 | 116.0 | 30.5 | 6,881 |
| 0.95 | 176.7 | 41.3 | 7,072 |
| 0.97 | 231.4 | 51.3 | 7,146 |

Going from 0.90 to 0.85 saves about 24% of the reviews for 3.0% less knowledge; 0.95 costs 52% more reviews for 2.8% more knowledge, and 0.97 doubles the reviews. The absolute numbers are lower than in an app that keeps every card, because the simulation retires cards whose stability passes 25 years, and its README is unclear about which parameter set it used; the relative effect is the robust part ([SSP-MMC-FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/SSP-MMC-FSRS.git)).

**Decision.** 0.90 by default. The setting offers the two named choices the research recommended, Lighter (0.85) and Intensive (0.95, for example before an exam), next to Balanced (0.90), and, also as recommended, values outside 0.80 to 0.95 are not accepted ([settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js), [Settings view](../app/src/views/settings.js)). In the app's own model (4.6), 0.85 instead of 0.90 lowers the daily answers at 10 new words a day with Listen + Speak by 16 to 21% in months one to three, 13% at six months and 11% after a year.

### 4.4 What the evidence says about learning words and tones

#### Tones: train the ear first, with real words and feedback (supported)

The best-supported practice in this research is high-variability phonetic training (HVPT): you hear many different words, say which tone you heard, and are told the answer at once. Its defining features are many voices, many phonetic contexts and corrective feedback ([Uchihara, Karas & Thomson 2025](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/studies-in-second-language-acquisition/article/high-variability-phonetic-training-hvpt-a-metaanalysis-of-l2-perceptual-training-studies/6ABB8C1F32D88D53EA8D05A4565E76F6)).

- In the founding Mandarin study, eight American learners had eight training sessions over two weeks with natural words from native speakers. Tone identification improved by about 21%; the gain carried over to new words (about 18%) and to new words from new voices (about 25%), and six months later it was still about 21% above the pretest ([Wang et al. 1999](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/106/6/3649/915818/Training-American-listeners-to-perceive-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext)). The length of a session was not seen (the verifier recalls about 40 minutes: **UNVERIFIED**).
- Ear training partly carries over to speaking. Native listeners identified the trainees' tones 18% more accurately after the training than before it, and their pitch contours moved closer to native ones ([Wang, Jongman & Sereno 2003](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/113/2/1033/545210/Acoustic-and-perceptual-evaluation-of-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext)). Across studies the gain is smaller for speaking than for listening: d = 0.54 against d = 0.92 ([Sakai & Moorman 2018](https://resolve.cambridge.org/core/journals/applied-psycholinguistics/article/can-perception-training-improve-the-production-of-second-language-phonemes-a-metaanalytic-review-of-25-years-of-perception-training-research/57401D28450902EE96659AD10AA11488)). A 2024 meta-analysis of 31 studies found small-to-medium gains in speaking, larger when learners were also given phonetic information (g = 0.94 against 0.45) ([Uchihara, Karas & Thomson 2024](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/applied-psycholinguistics/article/does-perceptual-high-variability-phonetic-training-improve-l2-speech-production-a-metaanalysis-of-perceptionproduction-connection/E38D8F5CE65DC708137B0E95F97C6BC7)).
- How large the effect is depends on how it is measured. A pooled g = 0.92 for the 2025 meta-analysis (79 studies) was seen only in a search summary and equals Sakai & Moorman's figure, so it is **UNVERIFIED**. Gains measured as before against after training are larger than gains over a control group, which one review puts at 0.28 to 0.67; the research plans with the smaller, controlled figures ([Uchihara, Karas & Thomson 2025](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/studies-in-second-language-acquisition/article/high-variability-phonetic-training-hvpt-a-metaanalysis-of-l2-perceptual-training-studies/6ABB8C1F32D88D53EA8D05A4565E76F6)).
- Limits: the Mandarin studies are small (8 trainees in 1999) and mostly lab tests on isolated syllables or words ([Wang et al. 1999](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/106/6/3649/915818/Training-American-listeners-to-perceive-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext)).

**From syllables to words.** Training on single syllables did not carry over to two-syllable words ([Silpachai 2020](https://benjamins.com/catalog/jslp.19010.sil)), and variation in context makes the first learning of tone contrasts harder ([Chang & Bowles 2015](https://cbchang.com/wp-content/uploads/ChangBowles2015_JASA.pdf)). Even advanced learners find tones hard, mainly because tones have to be used to recognise words, not because they cannot hear them ([Pelzl et al. 2019](https://resolve.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/BA813543C79288DF6B92E929442CB4BC/S0272263117000444a.pdf/advanced-second-language-learners-perception-of-lexical-tone-contrasts.pdf), [University of Maryland summary](https://linguistics.umd.edu/research?page=8)); another paper by the same group is titled "Even in the best-case scenario L2 learners have persistent difficulty perceiving and utilizing tones in Mandarin" ([SSLA](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/studies-in-second-language-acquisition/article/even-in-the-bestcase-scenario-l2-learners-have-persistent-difficulty-perceiving-and-utilizing-tones-in-mandarin/8316AA7FA2886AB59FC191CAD941602F/share/3d179cff0e5774987a1ec92e5129fa4086029fba)). No source seen compared tone-pair drills directly with single-syllable drills, so "single syllables briefly, then two-syllable words, then words in phrases" is an inference.

**Many voices or one** is disputed (**UNVERIFIED**). For: training with several voices beat training with one and was retained better after 6 months ([Silpachai 2020](https://benjamins.com/catalog/jslp.19010.sil)), and a 2021 meta-analysis reportedly found a small advantage (g = 0.28 right after training) ([Zhang, Cheng & Zhang 2021](https://pubs.asha.org/doi/10.1044/2021_JSLHR-21-00181); the figure is quoted second-hand by [Uchihara, Karas & Thomson 2025](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/studies-in-second-language-acquisition/article/high-variability-phonetic-training-hvpt-a-metaanalysis-of-l2-perceptual-training-studies/6ABB8C1F32D88D53EA8D05A4565E76F6)). Against, or only for some learners: 60 English speakers learning real Mandarin words showed no benefit ([Dong et al. 2019](https://peerj.com/articles/7191/), [PubMed Central copy](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6690337/)); two studies found that many voices helped learners with high aptitude and hindered the others ([Perrachione et al. 2011](https://people.bu.edu/tkp/Perrachione-Lee-Ha-Wong_2011_JASA.pdf), [Sadakata & McQueen 2014](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4243698/)); a registered replication with 166 participants (English r and l) found no difference in training ([Brekelmans et al. 2022](https://forrt.org/flora-replication-atlas/doi/10.1016/j.jml.2022.104352/)). The research follows the most recent synthesis, a 2025 network meta-analysis that found a moderate number of voices works best, without putting a number on "moderate" ([JSLHR 2025](https://pubs.asha.org/doi/10.1044/2024_JSLHR-24-00599)). The app's recordings come from audio-cmn, which has one voice per set: 1,707 syllables by Chen Wang and the HSK words by Yue Tan ([audio-cmn README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn/master/README.md)); which words use a recording, and which use the device voice or, once clips are made, a generated voice marked as synthetic, is counted in 2.3 ([import report](../data/hsk/import-report.json), [build-app-data.mjs](../scripts/build-app-data.mjs)).

**How to show tones.** With complete beginners, tone numbers, pitch contours and colours all helped, with a small practical advantage for contours and numbers over colour ([Godfroid, Lin & Ryu 2017](https://hub.hku.hk/handle/10722/244254)). In a class of first-year US university learners, contours shown with pinyin reduced tone errors more than contours alone and improved scores more than tone numbers with pinyin ([Liu et al. 2011](https://eric.ed.gov/?id=EJ945913)). Colour coding is popular in apps and among teachers and experienced learners ([Hacking Chinese](https://hackingchinese.com/?p=11523)) but was not better. The app shows pinyin with tone marks with every answer and a contour picture for each tone in the tone trainer; it does not colour-code tones ([tones.js](../app/src/views/tones.js)).

#### Pinyin before or with characters

Supported only indirectly: pinyin shown with tone information helps beginners learn tones ([Liu et al. 2011](https://eric.ed.gov/?id=EJ945913), [LearnLab summary](https://learnlab.org/research/wiki/index.php/Learning_a_tonal_language:_Chinese)). When to start characters was not researched with sources (**UNVERIFIED**): the research knows of a small literature on delaying characters but did not read it, and the verifier's impression is that experienced teachers disagree, some teaching speech through pinyin with characters as a separate course, others bringing characters in early (**UNVERIFIED**). Whether pinyin printed next to a character distracts from its shape is also **UNVERIFIED**.

**Decision: sound first.** Every card shows pinyin with tone marks once revealed. Characters are a separate card type, off by default, and on that card pinyin stays hidden unless you switch it on ([settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js)). This is a design choice for a learner whose goals are speaking and listening, not a research result.

#### Words alone or in sentences (**UNVERIFIED**)

The research's background knowledge, not checked against the studies: for a first meeting with a word, the word with its translation is at least as efficient as the word in a sentence; context is still needed for how the word is used, and should come once the word is known rather than instead of the pair; and deliberate flashcard study establishes words quickly but should not crowd out listening and speaking.

In the app, each word is first learned on its own (Listen, Speak); the In a phrase card comes later, for words that occur in one of the app's checked phrases; whole phrases are learned in the speaking scenes (section 3; [settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js), [study-items.js](../app/src/study-items.js)).

#### Recall or recognition (**UNVERIFIED**)

The research's background knowledge, not checked: recalling an answer (the testing effect) is among the most replicated findings in learning research, and practice transfers mostly in the direction practised, so recognising a word (Chinese to English) and producing it (English to Chinese) are both needed, with producing the harder of the two.

Partly supported: showing the right answer after every response. The Duolingo paper notes that "incorrect answers should be accompanied by explanations, not simply a “wrong” mark" ([Settles & Meeder 2016](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/duolingo/halflife-regression/master/settles.acl16.pdf)), and corrective feedback is one of the defining features of ear training ([Uchihara, Karas & Thomson 2025](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/studies-in-second-language-acquisition/article/high-variability-phonetic-training-hvpt-a-metaanalysis-of-l2-perceptual-training-studies/6ABB8C1F32D88D53EA8D05A4565E76F6)); the key experiment on feedback after errors was not read (**UNVERIFIED**).

In the app, Listen (audio to meaning) is recognition of the spoken word and Speak (English to Chinese, aloud) is recall and production; after every card the answer, pinyin and audio are shown ([study.js](../app/src/views/study.js)).

#### Spacing (supported)

Spreading practice over days rather than cramming is well established: the Duolingo paper says "the effects of spacing and lag are well-established in second language acquisition research", citing Cepeda et al. (2006) and older reviews ([Settles & Meeder 2016](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/duolingo/halflife-regression/master/settles.acl16.pdf)). Whether the gaps should grow or stay equal is contested and was not checked (**UNVERIFIED**); an adaptive scheduler sets the gap for each card and sidesteps the question. In the app, the card types of a word come on different days, and one session never asks the same word twice in different forms ([queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js)).

#### Shadowing (**UNVERIFIED**)

Shadowing (speaking along with a recording) was not checked: the studies the research knows of are mostly on Japanese learners of English, and no Mandarin tone shadowing study was read. One related, verified point: learners imitated tones better than they identified them ([Chun et al.](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/277655376_Acquisition_of_L2_Mandarin_Chinese_tones_with_learner-created_tone_visualizations)), so imitating well does not show that you know the tone. The app has no shadowing mode; the tone trainer's record-and-compare page (beta) shows your pitch next to the speaker's, and Speak cards can play your own recording, then the model (4.5; section 5).

#### Listening first

Supported for single items: hearing and identifying before speaking works and partly carries over to speaking (see the tone studies above). Whether to listen for weeks or months before speaking at all ("silent period" methods) was not checked: such methods are popular, and the research knows of no controlled studies backing them (**UNVERIFIED**). **Decision:** hear each item before you say it, and speak from the first week, because travel, family visits and work need speech soon.

#### Speaking feedback

Pronunciation teaching improves pronunciation on average (within-group d = 0.89 in a meta-analysis whose authors were not confirmed) ([meta-analysis](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/274178849_The_Effectiveness_of_Second_Language_Pronunciation_Instruction_A_Meta-Analysis)), and phonetic training overall reaches d = 0.762 ([JSLHR 2025](https://pubs.asha.org/doi/10.1044/2024_JSLHR-24-00432)), but neither isolates the effect of feedback. Visual feedback on the learner's own speech helped in small studies: every form of visual feedback tested improved L2 Spanish pronunciation ([Journal of Second Language Pronunciation](https://benjamins.com/catalog/jslp.20005.ols)), a real-time pitch display next to the model's improved French learners' prosody ([Hardison 2004](https://www.lltjournal.org/item/312/)), and a small Mandarin pilot found tone productions rated better after audio-visual training ([Wang 2012](https://www.igi-global.com/article/auditory-visual-training-mandarin-tones/67123/)). Recording yourself and comparing is therefore cheap, plausible practice rather than an evidence-backed method. Whether learners can judge their own pronunciation was not checked (**UNVERIFIED**; the research believes they judge it poorly). So self-grading in the app is for "did I remember it", not for tone accuracy; ask your partner to check your tones now and then. More in section 5.

#### Summary: what is supported, what is advice, what is merely popular

| Status | Practice |
| --- | --- |
| Supported by the sources read | Ear training for tones with immediate feedback ([Wang et al. 1999](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/106/6/3649/915818/Training-American-listeners-to-perceive-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext)); moving from syllables to real words ([Silpachai 2020](https://benjamins.com/catalog/jslp.19010.sil)); spacing over days ([Settles & Meeder 2016](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/duolingo/halflife-regression/master/settles.acl16.pdf)); tone marks or contour pictures with pinyin rather than colour alone ([Godfroid, Lin & Ryu 2017](https://hub.hku.hk/handle/10722/244254), [Liu et al. 2011](https://eric.ed.gov/?id=EJ945913)); FSRS predicting recall better than SM-2 ([srs-benchmark](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark/main/README.md), [srs-benchmark repository](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/srs-benchmark.git)) |
| Mixed evidence | Many voices rather than one; a moderate number did best in the newest synthesis ([JSLHR 2025](https://pubs.asha.org/doi/10.1044/2024_JSLHR-24-00599)) (disputed, **UNVERIFIED**) |
| Common advice from teachers and experienced learners, not checked | Drilling tone pairs, learning pinyin and tones early, shadowing, recording yourself; on when to start characters they seem to disagree (**UNVERIFIED**) |
| Popular, but not better when tested | Colour-coded tones ([Godfroid, Lin & Ryu 2017](https://hub.hku.hk/handle/10722/244254), [Hacking Chinese](https://hackingchinese.com/?p=11523)) |
| Popular, no controlled support known | Months of listening before speaking (**UNVERIFIED**) |
| Believed from learning research, not read here | The testing effect, word–translation pairs before sentences, practising both directions, spreading similar words over sessions (**UNVERIFIED**) |

### 4.5 The design that follows

**Change after use (November 2026).** The learner found the Listen-first order hard to use and asked to recognise words from the characters and recall them from the English instead. The default is now **Chinese → English (the Characters card) → English → Chinese (the Speak card) → In a phrase**, with Listen off (it can be switched back on) and a setting, also on the card, for showing pinyin under the characters ([settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js), [study.js](../app/src/views/study.js)). This is the learner's choice, not a research result; the reasoning below describes the original sound-first design. Saved settings from before the change are moved to the new defaults once.

**Original card order: Listen → Speak → Characters → In a phrase.** Each word gets one card per enabled type, introduced one type at a time: a new word starts with its first type, and the next type unlocks the day after the previous one was first studied ([queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js), [settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js)).

1. **Listen**: hear the word, recall the meaning, with an optional tone check before revealing (pick each syllable's tone as spoken, tone changes included). Hearing comes first because ear training works and partly carries over to speaking ([Wang et al. 1999](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/106/6/3649/915818/Training-American-listeners-to-perceive-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext), [Wang, Jongman & Sereno 2003](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/113/2/1033/545210/Acoustic-and-perceptual-evaluation-of-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext)). The research also notes leads, not read, that speaking too early can disrupt learning a new sound (**UNVERIFIED**).
2. **Speak**: see the English, say the word aloud (optionally recording yourself), reveal, hear the model (after your own recording, if you made one), grade yourself ([study.js](../app/src/views/study.js), [recorder.js](../app/src/recorder.js)). Speaking is the goal, and listening alone carries over only partly ([Sakai & Moorman 2018](https://resolve.cambridge.org/core/journals/applied-psycholinguistics/article/can-perception-training-improve-the-production-of-second-language-phonemes-a-metaanalytic-review-of-25-years-of-perception-training-research/57401D28450902EE96659AD10AA11488)).
3. **Characters**: see the characters, recall pinyin and meaning. Off by default; switch it on when you want to read, or for the HSK exam, which has a reading part at every level from 1 to 6 ([exam structure](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk)). It adds about half again as many answers (4.6).
4. **In a phrase**: a checked phrase with the word left out, only for words that occur in one.

Phrases from the speaking scenes get Listen, then Speak; family terms start with Speak ([settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js)). New words come in level order, most frequent first, and if scene or family decks are switched on, their items are mixed in turn by turn ([study-items.js](../app/src/study-items.js), [data.js](../app/src/data.js)). That follows the research's suggestion to introduce words by situation and spread similar words over sessions (**UNVERIFIED**).

**Tone module first.** The evidence points to a short ear-training phase before and alongside the first words, then a tone check that never stops. The research suggests about eight short identification sessions over two weeks (the dose of the 1999 study), single syllables for the first one or two, then two-syllable words from your own deck, with right/wrong feedback and replay after every answer ([Wang et al. 1999](https://pubs.aip.org/asa/jasa/article-abstract/106/6/3649/915818/Training-American-listeners-to-perceive-Mandarin?redirectedFrom=fulltext), [Silpachai 2020](https://benjamins.com/catalog/jslp.19010.sil)). Starting confusable pairs (such as tone 2 against tone 3) as a two-choice task rests on the verifier's memory of the 1999 training task (**UNVERIFIED**).

What the app has under Home → Practise → Tones ([tones.js](../app/src/views/tones.js), [tonedrill.js](../app/src/lib/tonedrill.js)):

- Single syllables: 20 per round, pick the tone, immediate feedback; marked "Start here".
- Two-syllable words: 20 per round, using only words spoken with their dictionary tones, so tone changes cannot make the answer ambiguous ([build-app-data.mjs](../scripts/build-app-data.mjs)).
- Minimal pairs: 12 per round; which of two words that differ only in tone was played.
- Rounds lean toward the tones you mix up, and the page suggests moving on to words at about 8 in 10 right. Every sound is a native recording; the device voice is never used here.
- The tone check on Listen cards (on by default, for words of up to three syllables) keeps tone identification in daily practice ([study.js](../app/src/views/study.js)).

Not yet: the app does not lay out the first two weeks as a tone course or remind you to do it (Home only labels Tones "Start here" until your first round, then names the suggested drill, [home.js](../app/src/views/home.js)), and each drill uses a single voice. The research suggests adding at least one more voice, for example labelled recordings of your partner and family, an idea that has not been tested ([audio-cmn README](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hugolpz/audio-cmn/master/README.md)).

**Defaults** ([settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js), [queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js)):

| Setting | Default | Why, and what you can change |
| --- | --- | --- |
| New words a day | 10 | The scheduler research suggested 5 for a beginner in short phone sessions (about 50 to 65 reviews and roughly 15 minutes a day after a year). The app uses 10: in the model, 10 with Listen + Speak means about 17 minutes a day after a month and 35 after a year, and all HSK 4 words met in 200 days instead of 400 (4.6). If that is too much time, set 5. FSRS works as well with 5 as with 50 new cards a day ([Anki documentation](https://github.com/ankitects/anki.git)), so this is a workload choice only. "Learn 5 more new" adds extra words on a given day. |
| Card types | Listen, Speak and In a phrase on; Characters off but available | speaking and listening first; Characters adds about half again as many answers |
| Desired retention | 0.90 | 4.3 |
| Maximum reviews a day | 300 | overdue cards wait for the next day; nothing is lost |
| Pause new words while reviews are overdue | on | after a missed day you catch up first, so the load does not snowball; "Learn 5 more new" still works |
| Daily goal | 50 answers | shown as today's answers against the goal on Home and Progress; the streak counts every day with at least one answer ([stats.js](../app/src/lib/stats.js), [home.js](../app/src/views/home.js)) |
| Active decks | HSK 1, 2 and 3 of the 2025 syllabus | change them under Decks |
| Study day | 04:00 to 04:00 | study after midnight, until 04:00, still counts for the day before |

Left for later: a guided first two weeks of tone training; a second voice for the drills; and the review log (4.2). Already in the app: the two named retention choices (4.3); pausing new words while reviews are overdue, which the research recommended (on by default); and the real study time, today and over the last 7 days, on the Progress page, from the time each answer takes ([settings.js](../app/src/lib/settings.js), [queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js), [progress.js](../app/src/views/progress.js), [stats.js](../app/src/lib/stats.js)).

### 4.6 A realistic plan

How long will it take? The numbers below come from a model, [scripts/simulate-plan.mjs](../scripts/simulate-plan.mjs), which runs the app's own scheduler code. Treat them as a guide to the size and shape of the work, not as a promise.

**How the model works.**

- FSRS-6 with default weights through the app's scheduler code (ts-fsrs 5.4.2), desired retention 0.90 (one run at 0.85), fuzz on, learning steps of 1 and 10 minutes.
- You add N new words a day, in order. Each word's card types arrive one per day, as in the app: Listen, then Speak the next day, then Characters. "1 card type" means Listen only, "2" Listen + Speak (the default), "3" Listen + Speak + Characters. The In a phrase card is not modelled.
- The simulated learner remembers each card with exactly the probability FSRS predicts, gets a new card right half the time on first sight, and presses only Again or Good.
- Every due card is done on its day: no missed days and no daily limits.
- 8 seconds per answer. "Answers" counts everything you answer: first showings, the same-day repeats after 1 and 10 minutes, and reviews.
- Adding words stops at 5,400, the end of HSK 6 in the 2025 syllabus. The runs cover 400 days with a fixed random seed, so they give the same numbers every time. To repeat one: `node scripts/simulate-plan.mjs --days 400 --types 2` (add `--retention 0.85` for the lighter run).

**Days until you have met every word of a level.** The 2025 syllabus has 300, 500, 1,000, 2,000, 3,600 and 5,400 words up to HSK 1 to 6 ([official syllabus](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk), [harukicoder/hsk30](https://github.com/harukicoder/hsk30)). The app's decks count distinct words and hold slightly fewer, 5,375 up to HSK 6 on 2026-11-02 ([manifest](../app/data/manifest.json); section 1). This table is the same for 1, 2 or 3 card types and for both retention values.

| New words a day | HSK 1 (300) | HSK 2 (500) | HSK 3 (1,000) | HSK 4 (2,000) | HSK 5 (3,600) | HSK 6 (5,400) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | 60 | 100 | 200 | 400 | 720\* | 1,080\* |
| 10 | 30 | 50 | 100 | 200 | 360 | 540\* |
| 15 | 20 | 34 | 67 | 134 | 240 | 360 |
| 20 | 15 | 25 | 50 | 100 | 180 | 270 |
| 30 | 10 | 17 | 34 | 67 | 120 | 180 |

\* Beyond the 400 simulated days; this is simply the number of words divided by the new words a day.

**Answers a day, with minutes at 8 seconds per answer in brackets.** First week = average of days 1–7; month 1 = days 22–28; month 3 = days 85–91; month 6 = days 176–182; year 1 = days 359–365; peak = the busiest single day in 400 days.

Listen only (1 card type per word):

| New words a day | First week | Month 1 | Month 3 | Month 6 | Year 1 | Peak |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | 17 (2) | 31 (4) | 42 (6) | 53 (7) | 63 (8) | 86 (11) |
| 10 | 37 (5) | 62 (8) | 87 (12) | 105 (14) | 132 (18) | 176 (23) |
| 15 | 53 (7) | 99 (13) | 132 (18) | 166 (22) | 164 (22) | 252 (34) |
| 20 | 75 (10) | 128 (17) | 185 (25) | 209 (28) | 85 (11) | 294 (39) |
| 30 | 117 (16) | 184 (25) | 281 (37) | 287 (38) | 78 (10) | 363 (48) |

| New words a day | First week | Month 1 | Month 3 | Month 6 | Year 1 | Peak |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | 32 (4) | 64 (9) | 90 (12) | 104 (14) | 136 (18) | 167 (22) |
| 10 | 69 (9) | 125 (17) | 184 (25) | 217 (29) | 259 (35) | 340 (45) |
| 15 | 104 (14) | 190 (25) | 268 (36) | 326 (43) | 334 (45) | 473 (63) |
| 20 | 142 (19) | 250 (33) | 367 (49) | 425 (57) | 170 (23) | 577 (77) |

| New words a day | First week | Month 1 | Month 3 | Month 6 | Year 1 | Peak |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | 47 (6) | 91 (12) | 137 (18) | 161 (21) | 191 (25) | 244 (33) |
| 10 | 98 (13) | 193 (26) | 285 (38) | 318 (42) | 408 (54) | 495 (66) |
| 15 | 145 (19) | 283 (38) | 399 (53) | 481 (64) | 510 (68) | 694 (93) |
| 20 | 187 (25) | 370 (49) | 535 (71) | 648 (86) | 256 (34) | 895 (119) |

Listen + Speak at desired retention 0.85:

| New words a day | First week | Month 1 | Month 3 | Month 6 | Year 1 | Peak |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 5 | 31 (4) | 53 (7) | 76 (10) | 87 (12) | 111 (15) | 148 (20) |
| 10 | 64 (9) | 105 (14) | 145 (19) | 188 (25) | 230 (31) | 273 (36) |
| 15 | 102 (14) | 162 (22) | 237 (32) | 273 (36) | 283 (38) | 395 (53) |
| 20 | 125 (17) | 214 (29) | 300 (40) | 345 (46) | 164 (22) | 477 (64) |
| 30 | 192 (26) | 342 (46) | 437 (58) | 490 (65) | 152 (20) | 609 (81) |

**How to read it.**

- "Days to HSK 4" is the day you meet the last HSK 4 word, not the day you know them all; on that day the newest words are only hours old. The app counts a word as learned once its first card has left the learning steps ([queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js)).
- The daily load builds up for months. At 5 and 10 new words a day it is still rising after a year; at 15 it grows only a little between six months and a year.
- At 20 and 30 a day the year-1 figure drops because every word up to HSK 6 has been introduced (on day 270 or 180) and the model then adds no new words; what remains is reviews.
- Going from Listen only to Listen + Speak roughly doubles the answers (+80% to +116%). Characters adds about half again (+32% to +65%; +42% to +58% at 10 a day).
- Minutes are answers × 8 seconds, which is an assumption. In the FSRS authors' simulator a review answered Good takes 7.33 seconds, one answered Again 23.0, and a new card's first answer 13.68 (Good) to 33.79 (Again) ([SSP-MMC-FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/SSP-MMC-FSRS.git)); saying words aloud also takes longer than tapping. Expect more minutes than shown.
- Desired retention 0.85 saves 7 to 21% of the answers at 10 a day (11% after a year). That is less than the 24% in the published 5-year simulation (4.3), probably because this model also counts first showings and same-day repeats, which cost the same at any retention.
- The size is in line with published rules of thumb. Anki: "If you are consistently learning 20 new cards a day, you can expect your daily reviews to be roughly about 200 cards/day" ([Anki manual](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ankitects/anki-manual/main/src/deck-options.md)); the FSRS authors' simulation averages 116 reviews a day over five years at 10 new cards a day ([SSP-MMC-FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/SSP-MMC-FSRS.git)). The model gives 132 answers a day after a year at 10 a day with Listen only, counting first showings and same-day repeats as well.

**What this means for you.**

1. **Start by sorting what you already know.** Until you study your first card, Home suggests sorting the HSK 1 words; every deck has "Sort: know / learn", which shows each word you have not started with its pinyin, meaning and audio. "I know it" marks the word as skipped: it is never scheduled and counts as known on the Progress page. "Learn it" keeps it ([deck.js](../app/src/views/deck.js), [home.js](../app/src/views/home.js), [queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js)). Skipped words are never tested, so only skip words you understand when you hear them and can say yourself.
2. **Every skipped word brings every later level closer.** At 10 a day, skipping 400 of the 500 words of HSK 1–2 means meeting the last HSK 2 word after about 10 days instead of 50, and the last HSK 4 word after about 160 days instead of 200. The daily load at a given pace stays about the same in the model, because it depends on how many new words you add each day, not on which ones.
3. **The default pace**, 10 new words a day with Listen + Speak, means about 125 answers (17 minutes at 8 seconds each) a day after a month, 184 (25 minutes) after three months, 217 (29 minutes) after six months and 259 (35 minutes) after a year. If that is too much, 5 a day roughly halves it (136 answers, 18 minutes, after a year), and desired retention 0.85 cuts it by about a tenth to a fifth.
4. **Switch on Characters** when you want to read, or before taking the HSK exam with its reading part ([exam structure](https://github.com/docrealm/docs-hsk)): about half again as many answers (408 a day, 54 minutes, after a year at 10 a day).
5. **Compare with reality now and then.** The Progress page shows how many days the next level will take at your actual pace over the last 14 days, or at your setting if there is no history yet, and how long you actually studied today and over the last 7 days ([progress.js](../app/src/views/progress.js), [stats.js](../app/src/lib/stats.js)).

**This is a model, not a promise.** It assumes that FSRS-6's default predictions are right for you, although the defaults were fitted on Anki users' reviews in general, not on learners of Mandarin ([ABC of FSRS](https://github.com/open-spaced-repetition/awesome-fsrs.wiki.git)); that you study every day and finish every due card; and that an answer takes 8 seconds. In the busiest rows the app also behaves differently from the model: it holds back reviews beyond 300 a day (the default limit) and then pauses new words until those overdue reviews are done, and it unlocks at most 20 follow-up card types a day, so above 20 new words a day with two card types, or above 10 with three, the later card types arrive more slowly than in the model ([queue.js](../app/src/lib/queue.js)). The scheduler research ran a similar simulation of its own, which its verifier re-implemented independently; it is still marked **UNVERIFIED** for the same reason: it is a model estimate, not a measurement.

## 5. Speaking practice technology

Can the app hear you speak and tell you whether your tones were right? This section looks at the three ways a web app on a phone could try, as checked on 2026-11-02: the browser's built-in speech recognition, a cloud pronunciation-scoring service, and pitch tracking inside the app itself.

The research produced 33 findings. A second, independent pass checked them against primary sources (browser compatibility data, the browsers' own source code, specifications, vendor SDKs, the Praat manual): 23 are verified and 10 remain **UNVERIFIED**. Most of the unverified ones concern vendor documentation, prices and research literature: web search was unavailable during the check, and many vendor and academic sites could not be reached.

**In short.** None of the three can reliably judge a beginner's tones on a phone today without sending the voice to a company (details and sources in 5.1 to 5.3). Version 1 therefore leaves the judgement to you and helps you judge: you grade your own spoken answers, you can record yourself and play your attempt straight after a native recording, and the tone trainer draws both pitch lines in one chart (an experimental Beta, with no score). Nothing you record leaves the phone.

| Option | What it can tell you | Where your voice goes | Main obstacle | In v1 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Browser speech recognition (5.1) | the characters it heard, never the tones ([Web Speech spec](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebAudio/web-speech-api/main/index.bs)) | Apple's or Google's speech service; on a phone the page cannot insist that it stays on the device ([MDN compat data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz)) | documented as unavailable in the installed iPhone app ([caniuse](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Fyrd/caniuse/main/features-json/speech-recognition.json)) | not used |
| Azure pronunciation assessment (5.2) | scores per sentence, word and sound; no tone score ([Azure SDK source](https://github.com/microsoft/cognitive-services-speech-sdk-js.git)) | uploaded to Microsoft | upload and GDPR review, a server holding the key, price unconfirmed | not used; test in v2 |
| Tencent SOE (5.2) | the tone it expected and the tone it heard, per word ([Tencent SDK](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TencentCloud/tencentcloud-sdk-nodejs/master/src/services/soe/v20180724/soe_models.ts)) | uploaded to Tencent | apparently mainland-China accounts only ([Tencent international SDK](https://github.com/TencentCloud/tencentcloud-sdk-nodejs-intl-en.git)) | not used |
| Pitch tracking in the app (5.3) | the shape of your pitch next to a native speaker's | stays on the phone | tracking errors on tones 3 and 4 and octave jumps ([Praat manual](https://github.com/praat/praat.git)), so no automatic score | built: Beta, no score |
| Self-grading (5.4) | your own judgement after hearing the model | nowhere | relies on your ear | built |

### 5.1 Speech recognition in the browser (Web Speech API)

The Web Speech API lets a web page ask the browser to turn speech into text. For Mandarin (`zh-CN`) the text is Chinese characters. Current versions on 2026-11-02 were Chrome and Chrome for Android 156, released 2026-10-20 ([MDN browser data](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/browser-compat-data/main/browsers/chrome.json)), and Safari and iOS 27.2, released 2026-10-26 ([MDN browser data](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/browser-compat-data/main/browsers/safari_ios.json)).

| Where | Available? | Where the audio is processed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| iPhone, Safari tab | Yes, under the prefixed name `webkitSpeechRecognition`, since iOS 14.5 ([MDN compat data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz)) | Apple's speech recogniser: on the phone when it supports on-device recognition for the language, otherwise possibly on Apple's servers ([WebKit source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/WebKit-7625.2.8/Source/WebCore/Modules/speech/cocoa/WebSpeechRecognizerTask.mm)). Whether Mandarin is recognised on the device on a given iPhone is **UNVERIFIED**. |
| iPhone, Home Screen app (installed) | Documented as not available ([caniuse](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Fyrd/caniuse/main/features-json/speech-recognition.json)). That note was written in May 2021 and never revised; the behaviour on iOS 26/27 is **UNVERIFIED**. | – |
| Android, Chrome tab | Yes: `SpeechRecognition` since Chrome 139, prefixed since Chrome 33 ([MDN compat data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz)) | Handed to Google's Android speech service (`com.google.android.tts` on Android 12 and later), which decides between phone and server ([Chromium Android backend](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/content/public/android/java/src/org/chromium/content/browser/SpeechRecognitionImpl.java)); Chromium never uses its own on-device mode on Android ([Chromium speech manager](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/content/browser/speech/speech_recognition_manager_impl.cc)). Where Mandarin is processed is **UNVERIFIED**. |
| Android, installed app | Probably as in a tab: Chromium's speech code does not check whether the app is installed ([Chromium speech manager](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/content/browser/speech/speech_recognition_manager_impl.cc)), but no device test or report was found: **UNVERIFIED**. | as above |
| Desktop Chrome (for comparison) | Yes. Only here can a page require on-device recognition (`processLocally`, Chrome 139+) after a one-time language-pack download ([MDN compat data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz), [MDN guide](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/content/main/files/en-us/web/api/web_speech_api/using_the_web_speech_api/index.md)); a Mandarin pack exists ([Chromium source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/components/soda/constants.h)). | Otherwise streamed to Google, so it "won't work offline" ([MDN](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/content/main/files/en-us/web/api/speechrecognition/index.md)). |

On the iPhone, Safari's engine runs several checks before recognition starts: the host app needs microphone and speech-recognition permission, Apple must have a recogniser for the language, the user must allow it, and the page must be visible ([WebKit source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/WebKit-7625.2.8/Source/WebKit/UIProcess/SpeechRecognitionPermissionManager.cpp)). The code cannot show whether the Home Screen app passes these checks; only a test on an iPhone can. The 2021 note also says Siri must be switched on ([caniuse](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Fyrd/caniuse/main/features-json/speech-recognition.json)).

The sources disagree on one point. caniuse (data updated 2026-10-28) still lists Chrome's support as prefixed only, while MDN's compatibility data and Chromium's own interface definition show the unprefixed name since Chrome 139 ([caniuse](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Fyrd/caniuse/main/features-json/speech-recognition.json), [MDN compat data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz), [Chromium source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/third_party/blink/renderer/modules/speech/speech_recognition.idl)). This section follows MDN and Chromium, because they are recorded per version and based on the browser's own code. Code would check for both names anyway.

**It never reports tones.** Each result is a short list of guesses, each just a text and a confidence number ([Web Speech spec](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebAudio/web-speech-api/main/index.bs)). There is no pinyin, tone, timing or pitch. The confidence means different things per browser: Safari reports the highest confidence of any segment ([WebKit source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/WebKit-7625.2.8/Source/WebCore/Modules/speech/cocoa/WebSpeechRecognizerTask.mm)), and Chrome on Android reports 1.0 whenever Google's service gives no value ([Chromium Android backend](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/content/public/android/java/src/org/chromium/content/browser/SpeechRecognitionImpl.java)), so it cannot serve as a quality score.

Turning the recognised characters back into pinyin only gives the dictionary tones of those characters, not the tones you produced. Homophones make this weaker still. In the Make Me a Hanzi character data, 9,565 characters share 1,208 syllable-and-tone combinations with tones 1 to 4 (1,236 with the neutral tone, built on 402 syllables), and 1,035 of the 1,208 belong to two or more characters (median 5, up to 80 for yì) ([Make Me a Hanzi](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/skishore/makemeahanzi/master/dictionary.txt)). The data mixes simplified, traditional and rare characters, so everyday ambiguity is smaller, but the point stands: 买 mǎi "buy" and 卖 mài "sell" differ only in tone.

Two likely problems were not measured and are **UNVERIFIED**. First, the recogniser runs in dictation mode and uses context ([WebKit source](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebKit/WebKit/WebKit-7625.2.8/Source/WebCore/Modules/speech/cocoa/WebSpeechRecognizerTask.mm)), so in 我想买… a wrongly toned *mai* may still come back as 买, and a matching transcript would hide the error. Second, single syllables are probably the hardest case: MDN's own single-word colour demo kept hearing "azure" as "as you" and "khaki" as "car key" ([MDN guide](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mdn/content/main/files/en-us/web/api/web_speech_api/using_the_web_speech_api/index.md)), and no figures for Mandarin were found.

Other limits. It works over HTTPS only ([Web Speech spec](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebAudio/web-speech-api/main/index.bs)). On Android, recognition stops when the page is hidden and stays stopped until the page starts it again ([Chromium speech manager](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chromium/chromium/main/content/browser/speech/speech_recognition_manager_impl.cc)). On phones the page cannot hand its own microphone stream to the recogniser (`start(MediaStreamTrack)` is desktop Chrome 135+ only) ([MDN compat data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz)), so recording yourself and recognition would be separate steps; whether both can run at the same time on a phone is **UNVERIFIED**.

Recognition running inside the page itself was also looked at. sherpa-onnx (Apache-2.0, npm 2.0.1, 15.45 MB unpacked) is a Node.js package; in a browser it needs the project's own WebAssembly builds and separately downloaded models ([npm sherpa-onnx](https://registry.npmjs.org/sherpa-onnx)). transformers.js 4.3.2 (Apache-2.0, 9.99 MB unpacked) runs in the browser but also downloads its models separately ([npm transformers.js](https://registry.npmjs.org/@huggingface%2ftransformers)). Both output text, not tones; neither was tested for Mandarin accuracy, download size or phone performance; and WebAssembly would need the CSP keyword `'wasm-unsafe-eval'` ([MDN compat data](https://registry.npmjs.org/@mdn/browser-compat-data/-/browser-compat-data-8.1.7.tgz), [CSP3](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/w3c/webappsec-csp/main/index.bs)).

**Decision: not in v1.** It cannot report tones, it is documented as unavailable in the installed iPhone app, the audio may go to Apple or Google without the page being able to prevent it, and on Android its confidence may be a constant 1.0 (sources above).

**In the app today:** there is no speech-recognition code. Apart from its own pitch tracker (5.4), the only speech technology the app uses is the device's speech synthesis, which reads out words and phrases that have no native recording (section 2). The build can swap in pre-generated synthetic clips, labelled "Generated voice (synthetic)", but none have been generated yet ([README](../README.md#audio)).

**Later:** possibly an optional "the recogniser heard: …" check in a browser tab, shown only where the browser supports it, after a test on both phones with HSK 1-2 words and tone minimal pairs such as 买/卖 and 妈/马.

### 5.2 Cloud pronunciation scoring

A pronunciation-scoring service receives your recording plus the text you were meant to say, and returns scores. Every such service needs the recording uploaded.

**Microsoft Azure AI Speech, pronunciation assessment**

- Mandarin works: Microsoft's official Python and JavaScript samples run scripted zh-CN assessment, first splitting the Chinese text into words ([Azure samples](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/cognitive-services-speech-sdk.git)).
- Scores: accuracy, fluency, completeness and an overall score, optionally a prosody score ("stress, intonation, speaking speed and rhythm"), plus an accuracy score and error type per word and results per sound (phoneme) ([Azure SDK source](https://github.com/microsoft/cognitive-services-speech-sdk-js.git)).
- Tones: there is no tone score. The SDK's result model has no tone field, and none of the official zh-CN samples reads one ([Azure SDK source](https://github.com/microsoft/cognitive-services-speech-sdk-js.git), [Azure samples](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/cognitive-services-speech-sdk.git)).
- **UNVERIFIED** (Microsoft's documentation was seen only through search summaries): the only intonation-related error type is "Monotone", and Microsoft's Mandarin phone set appends a tone number (for example "zh i 1"), so a wrong tone might lower the accuracy score ([Microsoft Learn: how-to](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/ai-services/speech-service/how-to-pronunciation-assessment), [Microsoft Learn: phone sets](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/ai-services/speech-service/speech-ssml-phonetic-sets)). Whether it does is not documented. A third-party design note reached the same conclusion and recommends testing correct against wrongly toned recordings ([GitHub issue](https://github.com/davadev/obsidian_chinese_comprehensible_input/issues/183)), and a hobby app that grades syllables with Azure's accuracy score plans a separate tone model on top ([GitHub pull request](https://github.com/johnhodgson140-ai/mandarin-learning/pull/3)); both are practitioners' notes and were not re-checked.
- Prosody score for Mandarin: **UNVERIFIED**. The documentation (seen through search) says prosody for free, unscripted speech is US English only; Microsoft's samples request prosody for zh-CN but also cope with getting none back ([Microsoft Learn, Chinese edition](https://learn.microsoft.com/zh-cn/azure/ai-services/speech-service/how-to-pronunciation-assessment?pivots=programming-language-csharp), [Azure samples](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/cognitive-services-speech-sdk.git)).
- What it takes: the subscription key must stay on a server. Microsoft's browser sample has a small server route that exchanges the key for a short-lived token ([Azure samples](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/cognitive-services-speech-sdk.git)); how long a token lasts is **UNVERIFIED**. The page would use the Speech SDK for JavaScript 1.53.0 (MIT, 5.6 MB unpacked) ([npm](https://registry.npmjs.org/microsoft-cognitiveservices-speech-sdk/-/microsoft-cognitiveservices-speech-sdk-1.53.0.tgz)) or send a 16 kHz WAV file to the REST endpoint ([Azure samples](https://github.com/Azure-Samples/cognitive-services-speech-sdk.git)). For this app that means a serverless function on the host (section 6) and `connect-src` entries in the Content-Security-Policy, because fetch and WebSocket connections fall under `connect-src` ([CSP3](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/w3c/webappsec-csp/main/index.bs)).

**Azure price: UNVERIFIED, and the sources disagree.**

| Source | What it says |
| --- | --- |
| [Azure pricing page](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/pricing/details/speech/) (seen through search; the prices load dynamically and were not visible) | pronunciation assessment is an add-on charged "per hour per feature" on top of real-time speech to text, billed per second; the free tier (F0) includes 5 audio hours of real-time transcription a month |
| [Quotas and limits](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/ai-services/speech-service/speech-services-quotas-and-limits) | the free tier allows 1 request at a time, and this cannot be changed |
| Third-party 2026 pages ([review](https://blocksentient.com/review/microsoft-azure-speech-service/), [comparison](https://api.toneperfect.app/blog/pronunciation-assessment-api-pricing-compared/)) | add-on \$0.30 per audio hour on top of about \$1 per hour, so about \$1.30 per hour |
| [Microsoft Q&A answer](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/answers/questions/5608069/pricing-and-usage-of-pronunciation-assessment-feat) | costs the same as standard speech to text |
| [Azure China](https://www.azure.cn/en-us/pricing/details/cognitive-services/) | CNY 3.05 per hour per feature (China region only) |

This section follows the official page's structure (a base price plus an add-on per audio hour), which carries more weight than a forum answer, but no dollar amount is confirmed. If \$1.30 per hour is right, 30 minutes of scored speech a month would cost about \$0.65. Whether the free hours also cover the add-on is unknown, and the feature is not offered through Azure's "fast transcription" ([Microsoft Q&A](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/answers/questions/5575747/how-to-use-the-azure-speech-to-text-fast-transcrip)).

**Tencent Cloud Smart Oral Evaluation (SOE, 智聆口语评测)** is the only service found that documents an explicit tone result. For Chinese, each word can report the tone it expected (RefTone) and the tone it heard (HypothesisTone), as −1 or 1 to 4, next to accuracy and fluency scores; audio must be 16 kHz, 16-bit mono and is not stored by default ([Tencent SDK](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/TencentCloud/tencentcloud-sdk-nodejs/master/src/services/soe/v20180724/soe_models.ts)). But SOE is missing from Tencent Cloud International's official SDKs, which do include speech recognition and synthesis, so it appears to need a mainland-China account. That is inferred from the SDK list, not stated by Tencent ([Tencent international SDK](https://github.com/TencentCloud/tencentcloud-sdk-nodejs-intl-en.git)). Price, contract and data-location terms, the meaning of −1, and accuracy on learners' speech are **UNVERIFIED**.

**Other services**

| Service | What was found | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| iFlytek ISE (讯飞语音评测) | the official SDK has a Chinese evaluation client that streams to a mainland-China domain, xfyun.cn ([iFlytek SDK](https://files.pythonhosted.org/packages/f7/4e/e512b16dfe41a9d0c4162ee984ac4c13cd5d5c5363779407d92e82011407/xfyunsdkspeech-0.0.4-py3-none-any.whl)); tone scores, EU availability and price not found | **UNVERIFIED** |
| SpeechSuper | lists Chinese among its languages; no tone documentation, no prices, keys through a contact form ([SpeechSuper samples](https://github.com/speechsuper/SpeechSuper-API-Samples.git)) | **UNVERIFIED** |
| Speechace | no sign of Mandarin: pronunciation scoring in US and UK English, task scoring also in French and Spanish ([Speechace samples](https://github.com/speechace/speechace-api-samples.git), [Speechace SDK](https://registry.npmjs.org/speechaceapi/-/speechaceapi-1.0.0.tgz)) | **UNVERIFIED** for late 2026 |
| Google Cloud | no pronunciation-assessment product on its Speech-to-Text price list; transcription only, \$0.016 per minute for standard recognition (first 500,000 minutes a month) ([Google Cloud pricing](https://cloud.google.com/speech-to-text/pricing)) | verified |

**Privacy.** All of these upload your voice. Even the Web Speech specification warns that speech "might be sent to a remote server" and that developers need to be aware of the privacy implications ([Web Speech spec](https://raw.githubusercontent.com/WebAudio/web-speech-api/main/index.bs)). Cloud scoring also needs a network connection, a server holding the key and a per-hour price. The research flags that, for a user in the Netherlands, uploading voice recordings would need a GDPR review first: the lawful basis or consent, a processor agreement, and where the data is processed. The legal side itself was not researched.

**Decision: no cloud scoring in v1.** Uploading voice recordings needs a GDPR review and a server holding a key, the price is unconfirmed, Azure gives no tone score, and Tencent, the one service with tone results, is probably not available to an EU customer (sources above).

**In the app today:** the app calls no speech service. Its Content-Security-Policy allows connections only to its own server (`connect-src 'self'` in [app/vercel.json](../app/vercel.json)), so a cloud scorer would need a server function and a policy change.

**Later (a v2 test):** record deliberate wrong-tone minimal pairs and check whether Azure's zh-CN accuracy score drops, and whether a prosody score comes back for zh-CN; confirm the price in an EU region; do the GDPR review first. Tencent stays a long shot.

### 5.3 Pitch tracking in the app

Pitch (F0, the fundamental frequency) is how high or low the voice is at each moment, and a Mandarin tone is a pitch shape.
